You turn your head toward the ditch and raise the clan sign, thumb tucked into the fist: *I'm well, go.* Then, loud enough to carry, you tell Ushk to take them home to Harrowmere and see them to Veshra's door, and to tell her Korvash wore her charm and will be at her table when he can.

He barks for ten spears, and the Tally line splits, ten cloaks marching past you to the ditch. Ninna is already on her feet, counting heads aloud in a clear small voice, and the children rise out of the grass in a long ragged line behind her. The older ones carry the younger. None of them look back at you, because you told them not to, and you have never been prouder of anything.

In the ditch, Tessa's hood turns toward you. Her hand comes up from the grass. It is not the sign you gave. Her palm is flat and level, held steady: *grey. Hold. Wait.* She does not follow Ushk's column, and she does not rise. She sinks back into the dry grass as the children pass over her and is gone from sight.

On the ridge the grey cloak turns, and begins to move east along the skyline toward Greyshaft, hurrying to carry the news.

Gorrak watches the children go until the last small shape is over the rise. Then he says, "Walk," and the column swings north behind you, the mountains ahead, your wrists in iron, and the sun well past noon. You are at 5 hit points, unarmed, with Dargan's charm at your throat. Greyshaft is three days' road, and midwinter's eve is tomorrow.

What does Korvash do?

"The key is on a chain at the Foreman's belt. It has never left it, not in my hearing, and I have been his debtor six years." The rod taps once against his thigh. "My own name is written in that chest, with a number beside it that does not go down. Burn the book and the number burns. That has occurred to me more than once, and I have not liked what it did to my sleep."

A hundred paces pass. "He will not hang you. A hand that burned his copy is worth more to him alive, and he will want it seen. Tomorrow at dusk he moves the ledger, the Tin Row's leavings and the rest to the deep shaft, and you will go down with it. The song is that a debt is paid in the dark."

You ask about the walk. He tells you the old ore road runs over the shoulder of the mountain, a hard night's march, and if the column does not stop it will bring you to the lower gate at dusk tomorrow, in time. "I mean to be in time," he says, and nothing else, and you cannot tell whether that is a threat or a promise.

The afternoon lengthens. The road climbs, the grass thins to stone, and the chain between your wrists clinks with every step. Ushk's spears are long gone behind the rise. The light is going amber. A little before six, Gorrak raises a fist and the column halts beside a cleft of rock with a shelter's roof of overhang and a ring of old fire-stones. He orders a short halt, two hours, a fire without flames, water passed along.

You are at 5 hit points, manacled, with Dargan's charm at your throat. Greyshaft is a night's march away, and nobody has yet taken the iron helm off your pack. What does Korvash do?

DM: Cold iron under your cheek, a boot an inch from your face, and a sound like a bell struck underwater. You come up out of the dark on a hot breath, and you know before your eyes focus what you've been given: one hit point, the whole of it.

It is the third heartbeat of the fight. The manacle chain between your wrists hangs in two cut halves, one cuff still on each wrist. Gorrak Vael lies sprawled a few paces off on the slate, mask askew, his cloak dark with blood. He is down but not dead; you can see his chest move. The three Stair Guards who cut him down have turned back toward you, and the other three are crowding up the steps behind them with spears reversed for the butt.

The Foreman has stepped back into the shelter of his guards with a hand pressed to his ribs, where Gorrak's blade opened him. He is hurt, and he is smiling thinly, and the heavy key chain has gone back onto his belt. The iron-bound chest still sits on its litter beside him, four paces from you, and two of his guards are lifting it.

On the wall above, a single red cloth is still snapping in the wind. Nothing else moves up there.

You are at 1 hit point with no weapon, no rage, a breath of fire in your chest and your sister somewhere in the dark. It is your turn. What does Korvash do?

  [game] notebook: updated The Greyshaft Foreman
DM: You fill your lungs and the fire goes out of you in a low red cone across the Foreman, the two hobgoblins hauling the chest, and the litter's dry wood. The Foreman twists aside with a hiss and a scorched sleeve. The two bearers take it full in the face, and the litter flares. The chest's iron bands blacken, but the ledger inside it is another matter. You roar to Tessa to burn it down.

The reply is the flat edge of a longsword. The first guard misses you and the second does not. The blow comes down on the hit point you had left, and the world goes out like a lamp.

You come back in the dark, shackled by both wrists and ankles to a ring in a wall of cold wet rock, with your head ringing and a dull weight on your chest. The air smells of lamp-smoke, tin and fear. It is a little after seven in the evening, and you are at 1 hit point. The Foreman's guards have taken your cloak and helm, and the horn charm is gone from your neck. Someone, you suspect, took it as a keepsake.

You are in the deep shaft. It is a wide low gallery cut into the mountain, lit by two sputtering torches, with a heavy door at one end. Along the wall in the dim, shackled in a long row of their own, sit the last of the Tin Row's hands, thirty or forty children, silent, watching you. At the far end of the gallery a litter rests on the floor with a scorched chest on it, its iron bands blackened and its lid shut. Two Tally-sworn stand beside it, and the one nearest has a patch of singed beard.

Gorrak Vael is not here. You do not know if he is alive. Of Tessa there is no sign, no song, and no cloth. The only sound is a slow drip, and far above, very faint, the dusk bell, ringing again.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 71 ==
PLAYER: I keep still and low, eyes half-lidded on the two guards at the chest, and whisper to the nearest child down the row, "I'm Korvash, I walked ninety-four of your friends out of the Tin Row, and I won't leave you here. Did you see where they took the big captain in the tin mask, or a hooded girl with a broken horn?"

Then, very quietly under the drip of water, I begin humming the goat-counting song, so that if Tessa is anywhere in these rocks she'll know where to find us.
  [game] dice Korvash Dexterity (Stealth) whispering and humming unnoticed by the two guards at the chest, vs passive Perception 10: 1d20+1 = 1d20 [17] + 1 = 18
  [game] time: 20 minutes later: Day 20, 7:30:30 PM
  [DM write_lore (hidden)] {"entry": "Tessa", "add_to_description": "Day 20 ~7:30 PM: Korvash hummed the goat-counting song in the deep shaft, unnoticed by guards. A child said Gorrak was dragged up the third-gate stair by two grey cloaks; no one saw a hooded girl taken."}
  [game] notebook: updated Tessa
DM: The whisper passes unnoticed. The two guards at the chest are arguing in low Goblin about whose turn it is to hold the torch, and the drip covers the rest. The boy beside you, ten at most, with a split lip and a front tooth missing, stares at you a long moment before he answers in a voice like a cricket's.

"I know you. You're the big one from the gallery door." He swallows. "They dragged the mask-man up the third-gate stair, two grey cloaks with him. He wasn't moving. The Foreman went up first, holding his side, and he was *cross*." He glances at the guards, lowers his voice further. "I didn't see a girl with a broken horn. But there was a girl on the wall with a red cloth, before they shut the door. She was fast. I saw her go over the parapet, and nobody shot her."

You begin to hum. The goat-counting song, low and rough, the first verse and the second, under the drip. You are on the sixth goat when it comes back to you through the rock: three soft taps, very faint, then a pause, then three more. The rhythm is the counting song's, the seventh goat's, and it comes from the far wall of the gallery, behind the row of children, where an old black seam of rock runs from floor to roof. Nothing else moves. The guards don't turn.

It is half past seven. Above you, the slow bells ring the hours of midwinter's eve, and the chest sits on its litter, its iron bands scorched, thirty feet away with two guards beside it. You are at 1 hit point, shackled at wrist and ankle to a ring in the wall, with the key nowhere in reach.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 72 ==
PLAYER: I answer on the iron itself, tapping the ring with my shackle chain to the last two lines of the song, timed to fall in the gaps between the drips so the guards hear only water. Then I whisper to the boy, "Pass it down the row: whatever happens, nobody cries out, and when the wall opens, the littlest go first."

I keep my eyes on the two guards and the chest, counting their breaths and their torch changes, ready for the moment Tessa makes her move.

  [game] notebook: updated Tessa
DM: You tap the ring with your chain, the last two lines of the song, one tap to the gap between drips, so that the guards hear only water. Down the row the whisper goes, nudge by nudge, the way it goes in any herd: nobody cries out, and when the wall opens the littlest go first.

You get your answer sooner than you hoped. At the far end, where the black seam of rock meets the floor, a crack of darker dark widens by the width of a hand, and a small shape slides out of it on its belly, its hood pulled low, and flattens itself among the children. Tessa. She crawls along the base of the wall to you, never standing, and her fingers find the lock on your left wrist and set to work, a hair-thin pick clicking in the dark.

It does not go well. The first lock fights her, and the pick skates off the inside of the cuff with a scrape like a fingernail on slate. It's loud, quite loud, in the stillness of the shaft. Tessa freezes with her breath in her teeth.

At the far end one of the guards looks up, the one with the singed beard. He says something short in Goblin to his partner, picks the torch off its hook, and starts down the row between the children, slowly, with his hand on his sword hilt. Eighteen paces off. Twelve.

The torchlight swings across the wall and the children, and across you, one hit point and two cuffs, with Tessa's hood hunched against your ribs. The ring bolts you to the wall. The first lock is half-picked, and the boy beside you has gone absolutely still.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 73 ==
PLAYER: I roll over onto my side so my bulk hides Tessa against the wall and start to groan and rattle my chains like a man in a fever, muttering in Draconic about my father's goats. The guard has been told I'm half-dead, and I mean him to believe it.

My half-picked hand stays hidden under me, and if he bends down to check on me, I grab his sword arm and drag him in against the wall.

  [game] notebook: updated Tessa
DM: You roll onto your side so your bulk covers Tessa against the wall and let the fever take you. You rattle the chain, groan, and mutter in Draconic about your father's goats, and about the seventh one who never came home. Too much. You hear it as you do it, the way a bad actor hears himself, and so does he.

The guard stops a long pace short, just beyond the reach of your chain. His torch comes up. The light slides along the wall and over your shoulder, and finds a small rust-red hand with a hair-thin pick still in the lock of your cuff.

"Sick men," he says slowly, in Goblin, and the sword comes out of its scabbard with a soft ring, "don't mutter in Draconic about *goats*."

His partner at the chest has turned. The boy beside you has stopped breathing. Tessa has gone absolutely still against your ribs, one hand on the pick and the other on a small knife at her belt. You can see the guard's chest swell for the shout, and his eyes flick to the door. You are at 1 hit point, chained at both wrists and ankles. One cuff is half-picked, and he is a pace and a half away.

You have a heartbeat. What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 74 ==
PLAYER: "Tess, now!" I wrench the half-picked cuff with every ounce of strength I have left, straining the lock until it bursts, and lunge out the length of the chain to hook the guard's ankle with my free hand and drag him down, away from the door and the shout.

If I can, I'll smother his torch under him, so the shaft goes dark and the children hear Tessa's knife and my voice: "Littlest first, through the seam!"

  [game] notebook: updated Tessa
DM: You never see the blow that ends it. You go under in the dark of the shaft with Tessa's voice in your ear, and the last thing you know is her knife, small and bright, and a guard's face cut open with rage. The next thing you know is a candle.

You come back slowly, on a hard floor, to the smell of beeswax and lamp-oil and old paper. It is a little before ten at night, and you are at 1 hit point. Your wrists are shackled again to an iron ring let into a heavy table-leg, and your ankles are hobbled. You are in a long low room under a beamed roof, shuttered against the wind, and a stove is ticking in the corner. The master ledger lies open on a desk under a lamp. Beside it sits the iron-bound chest from the litter, its lid blackened but whole.

Tessa lies on the floor a pace from you with her hood thrown back, breathing, her wrists bound in front of her and her left forearm bandaged over the old scar. She is not awake. A few feet beyond her Gorrak Vael lies on his side on a pallet, trussed with cord, his mask gone. His face is the one you have never seen but once: greying, jaw to ear a pale seam of burn. His eyes are open and are fixed on you.

Behind the desk the Foreman writes. His side is bandaged tight under the apron, his soft hands are steady, and the brass beads click at the end of each line. He does not look up until he has finished the page. "Emberhide," he says mildly. "Two lines tonight. Hand: one. Mender: one. The captain I have not yet decided on." He blots the page and turns the ledger a little so that you can see it. Your name is there, in a neat clerk's hand, with the date, and beside it a number. Beneath it, Tessa's.

"You burned my copy and you hurt my side, and I should be angry with you," he says, in the same patient tone. "But I find I'm not. A hand that fights is worth three that don't. I will ask you one question, and I would like you to answer it truthfully, because it concerns your sister, and she is not strong." He lays down the pen and folds his hands over the beads. "Where is the rest of the ninety-four?"

Six Tally-sworn stand along the walls, spears upright, and one of them has a singed beard. The door at the far end is barred. The wind mutters at the shutters. Gorrak has not moved a muscle, but his bound hands have found each other behind his back.

You are at 1 hit point, with no rage and no breath to spend. Nothing in the room is yours. What does Korvash say or do?

== ROUND 77 ==
PLAYER: I lift my head and meet his flat eyes, and I give him my loudest, hoarsest laugh. "You've got a ledger full of numbers, Foreman, so count them yourself, because you'll not get the ninety-four from me, and I'd bite my own tongue out before I told you which road they took."

Then, lower: "Take what you want from me, the axe, the year, my neck, but touch Tessa and I'll find you in whatever dark you crawl into, even with my hands chained. Cut her cords and let her sleep, and then we'll talk."

  [game] notebook: updated The Greyshaft Foreman
DM: You give him the loudest, hoarsest laugh you have, and it rattles off the beams. You tell him he can count the ninety-four himself. You tell him you'd bite out your own tongue before naming a road, and that if he lays a finger on Tessa, you will find him in whatever dark he crawls into with your hands chained. You mean every word.

It lands wrong. You hear it land wrong. The laugh cracks halfway, the threat comes out of a throat gone raw, and what the room hears is a bleeding man in irons shouting at a table. The singed guard snorts. The Foreman only waits for the echo to die, folds his hands on the beads, and when he speaks, his voice is as soft as before.

"Bite out your tongue. That would be a waste, and I do not like waste." He nods slightly at the shuttered window. "I do not need the road, Emberhide. My scouts counted them. Ninety-four small hands, and Ushk's ten spears, and a soft-hearted sergeant leading them to your aunt's door. They are two days from the Border Stones, and I have a rider who can be at the Stones in one." He lets that sit. "I would prefer not to send him. It is a messy business, and Harrowmere has herders with bows."

The beads click once. "So. A bargain, since you are a man who keeps his word. I know that of you. Gorrak told me, six years of his dull reports. You will swear to me, on your father's blood, as you swore to him, that you will serve Greyshaft as a hand until I judge the count of ninety-four made good. I will judge it fairly, and I do not hurry. In return I cut your sister's cords tonight, send her to Harrowmere with a safe-conduct and a mule, and call back my rider. The captain I leave to you." His eyes slide to Gorrak. "I am told he is dear to you now."

On the floor Tessa stirs, once, and a small sound comes out of her, half a breath, half a word. Gorrak has turned his face to the wall.

You have given the Tallies a year and a day and have eleven months of it still owing. You are at 1 hit point, in chains, and the Foreman is waiting with his pen already raised over a fresh line. What does Korvash say or do?

== ROUND 78 ==
PLAYER: I hold his gaze. "I'll swear, Foreman, but not to an open-ended count you alone can judge, because that's a chain with no end. Write it in your own ledger with a fixed term: a year and a day, the same year I already owe the Tallies, and then I'm free of Greyshaft."

"Cut Tessa's cords now, call back your rider in front of me, and loose Gorrak, and I'll swear it on Dargan's blood. Her safe-conduct and a mule go first, and then my word."

  [game] notebook: updated The Greyshaft Foreman
DM: You hold his gaze and put it plainly: no open-ended count that only he can judge, because that is a chain with no end. A fixed term, a year and a day, written in his own hand. Tessa's cords cut, the rider called back before your eyes, Gorrak loosed, and then you will swear on Dargan's blood.

The Foreman considers it the way a clerk considers a rounding error: with real interest, and with no heat at all. The beads stop. The pen stays still. "A fixed term," he says slowly, "is tidy. I like tidy. I accept it, Emberhide. A year and a day, written, signed, and sealed with the oath you swore the Tallies." He dips the pen. "But I am not a fool, and you are already owed to Captain Vael for eleven months, and I do not buy what is already sold. Mine begins when his ends. A year and a day after the Tallies release you. That is two years, near enough, but each day is counted and each day has an end." He writes a line as he talks. "Your sister's cords I will cut tonight, with a mule and a safe-conduct, the moment the oath is spoken. The rider I recall when the ink has dried. And Captain Vael," he lifts his eyes to the man on the pallet, "stays in my ledger as surety, and walks out with you on the last day of your term, if you have served it well. If you do not, he does not."

Gorrak's eyes shut. Whatever he thinks, none of it shows on his burned face.

A hoarse voice comes from the floor. "Kor." Tessa's eyes are open, amber and dull with pain, fixed on you, and her bound hands are shaking. "Kor, *don't*. I know that voice. It writes a number next to every one. It never ends."

The six Tally-sworn along the wall have not moved. The Foreman's pen hovers above the line. The wind shifts at the shutters, and the stove ticks. You have 1 hit point, a sister waking in chains, and a man across the table who wants an oath from you more than he wants your axe.

What does Korvash say or do?

== ROUND 79 ==
PLAYER: I turn my head to Tessa and say gently, "Hush, Tess, I hear you. I'm not swearing to a word I haven't read."

Then to the Foreman, level and unhurried: "Read me every line before I speak a word of it, aloud, in Common, and put your pen to the page in front of me. Cut her cords first, as you promised, and then I'll hear the terms and judge them as a man who keeps his word."

  [game] notebook: updated The Greyshaft Foreman
DM: "Hush, Tess," you say, gently, over your shoulder. "I hear you. I won't swear to a word I haven't read." Then you turn back to the table and ask for it all, aloud, in Common, with his pen on the page in front of you, and her cords cut first.

The Foreman raises one eyebrow, pleased, in the way a teacher is pleased by a clever pupil. He says a word to the nearest Tally-sworn, who crosses the room with a knife, cuts Tessa's cords, and steps back. She doesn't run; she sits up against the leg of the table with her wrists in her lap, rubbing her fingers, and her eyes stay on you.

Then he reads, his voice precise and level, a clerk's voice. A term of one year and one day in the service of Greyshaft, beginning at the end of the Tallies' term. Service wherever the Foreman should send, in mine or yard or on the road. The captain Gorrak Vael as surety. The mender freed with a mule and a safe-conduct, upon the oath. The hand to count each day of service toward his term, and the Foreman to keep the count.

You listen to the shape of the words as much as to the words, the way you would listen to a goat-trader, and you catch it halfway through the third clause, in the small flat phrase he lets his voice slide over: *the term to run only while the hand is whole and obedient.* Whole. Obedient. Any day you were wounded, sick or sulking, any day the Foreman judged you disobedient, would not be counted. You would be a hand for as long as a hobgoblin with a pen chose to say so, and with a Tally-sworn sergeant to break a bone whenever the count ran short.

You say so, flatly, and read the clause back to him word for word.

The Foreman does not flinch. He sets down the pen. "You hear well." For the first time he looks, very slightly, annoyed. "It is a common clause. I strike it, if you insist, but I want it understood that it was meant honestly. A mine cannot count the days a man lies in bed." He picks the pen up again and draws one neat line through the words. "And the rest?"

Tessa has not taken her eyes off you. Gorrak's single open eye is watching the Foreman's hand. The six spears along the wall have not shifted. It is a little after ten at night, and you have 1 hit point and a contract with one trick struck out, with the pen waiting for your answer.

What does Korvash say or do?
  [game] conversation shortened: the DM's summary of turns 0 to 63, then turns 64 to 83

== ROUND 80 ==
PLAYER: I nod slowly, as if weighing it. "Good. Now read me the rest, Foreman, the surety, the safe-conduct, the mule, every word."

While he draws breath to read, I say in Draconic to Tessa, low and flat, as though speaking to myself about the seventh goat: "When I shout 'home,' the lamp, Tess, over onto the ledger, and run for the children." Then I fix my eyes on the Foreman's pen, so the six spears watch me and not her.
  [game] dice Korvash Deception: murmuring Draconic to Tessa as if to himself while the guards watch him, DC 12 (none of the room speaks Draconic, but a guard may sense a ploy): 1d20+1 = 1d20 [16] + 1 = 17
  [game] dice Foreman's Insight (passive-style check) on whether Korvash's murmuring is a ploy: 1d20+2 = 1d20 [16] + 2 = 18
  [game] time: 8 minutes later: Day 20, 10:09:12 PM
DM: You murmur it low and flat in Draconic, as a man mutters to himself when he's tired and frightened. Six of the seven hobgoblins in the room hear only a prisoner talking to his goats, and one of them yawns. Tessa doesn't look at you. Her thumb presses once against the table leg, the clan's *yes*, and her eyes drift to the lamp.

The Foreman is the seventh, and he stops mid-breath. He doesn't know the tongue. He knows what it sounds like when a prisoner has finished weighing his terms and started weighing the room. His eyes rest on you a moment, then on Tessa, soft and unhurried.

"Draconic," he says mildly. "A pretty language. I regret not having it." He lifts a finger. The nearest Tally-sworn, a lean grey-eyed hobgoblin, steps behind Tessa and lowers his spear until the point rests against the stone beside her shoulder. With the other hand the Foreman slides the lamp across the desk, past the ledger and out of her reach, to stand at his own elbow. It is a small, tidy move, made without any haste.

"Where was I," he says. "The rest." He reads on, in the same level voice. The surety is Captain Vael, held in the Foreman's keeping and released on the last day of your term. The mender is freed upon the oath with one mule, a safe-conduct to Harrowmere's gate, and three days' bread. The rider is recalled when the ink has dried. The count is kept in the Foreman's ledger and may be read by the hand on request, once a season. You listen for tricks the way you'd listen for a loose stone on a goat-track, and you find none. He has struck the one he was caught on and kept the others honest, because honest terms cost him nothing.

He finishes and lays the pen across the page. The lamp burns a long arm's length from Tessa, with the ledger beside it and the Foreman's soft hands on either side. Six spears stand along the walls, and the grey-eyed one is at Tessa's back. Gorrak lies on his pallet with his eye open, watching the pen. Your wrists are still chained to the table leg, and you have 1 hit point.

"That is all of it," the Foreman says. "Will you swear?"

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 81 ==
PLAYER: "I'll swear," I say, and I plant my feet, take hold of the table leg that holds my chains, and begin loud and slow: "On Dargan's blood, and the blood of my clan..."

On the word I haul up on the table with every scrap of strength I have and roar "HOME!", heaving it over toward the Foreman so the lamp, the pen and the open ledger crash down together in a spill of burning oil. "Tess, the book, burn it, then the children!"

Your legs are shackled and your hands are chained to the table. The table is heavy oak, and it rises barely a hand's breadth before it stops. The roar "HOME!" is already out of you. It shakes the shutters.

The Foreman has been waiting for it. The moment you drew breath he was out of his chair, the open ledger clutched to his chest, backing toward the barred door with a guard's shoulder between you. Tessa moved too, faster than you've ever seen her move. She snatched the lamp off the desk and hurled it, and it broke on the flagstones short of him in a sheet of burning oil. The flames licked his hem and the corner of the ledger and nothing more. Then the grey-eyed guard's sword pommel came down on the back of her skull, and she folded.

You never see the blow that takes you. A guard's boot is on your chain, a hilt comes down, and the lamp-bright room goes out.

You wake slowly. The smell of burnt oil and singed paper is thick in the air, and the shutters are still closed. It is a little after eleven at night, and you have 1 hit point. The table has been righted, and a black scorch runs across the floor in front of the desk. Your wrists are shackled to the table leg again, harder than before, and the iron has been wired shut at the cuffs.

Tessa sits on a stool by the stove, awake, with her hands bound and a guard's spear across her knees. A dark knot is rising at the back of her skull. She looks at you and shakes her head, once, very slightly: *I'm sorry.* Gorrak lies on his pallet where he was, and his burned face is turned to the ceiling.

The Foreman sits behind the desk with the ledger open before him. Its lower corner is brown and curled, a hand's width of the last pages scorched. He does not look angry. He is turning the page with a clean cloth, and the brass beads lie still beside the pen. The contract lies in front of you, the fresh ink dry.

"You are a man of your word," he says gently. "It was an ugly way to find out, but I am glad I did not need to break anything permanent. Your sister lives, as you see. My rider is still waiting at the Stones for a word from me." He dips the pen. "My terms are changed, Emberhide. The mule and the safe-conduct stay in the stable. Your sister remains here as the mender, a month's service for every day you lived in defiance. Swear, and she is cared for and fed, and so is the captain."

The six spears along the wall are all still standing. The singed guard watches you with something close to a smile.

You are at 1 hit point, chained and wired shut. Your sister's life is on the table with the pen, and the Foreman has started a new line.

What does Korvash say or do?

DM: You let your head sag and your voice go rough, and you say it without a trace of the shouting man in it. No more tricks. You name your price: the rider's recall written before the oath is spoken, Tessa's hands cut free and no one touching her, and a healer for Gorrak. You put the rest in your eyes, for her alone.

Tessa's chin lifts a hair. She doesn't answer, but her bound hands go still in her lap.

The Foreman studies you a long moment, the way a clerk studies a column that has come out too neatly. Then he nods. "Reasonable. Tidy. I dislike untidy oaths, and a man swearing with a grudge is an untidy thing." He dips the pen and writes without hurry, saying the words aloud. A rider is to turn back from the Border Stones and make no hand against the herd of Harrowmere. He sands the sheet, folds it, and presses a seal into the wax. One of the guards takes it and goes out through the barred door, and you hear the bar fall behind him. "There," says the Foreman. "Done before you speak."

He gestures, and the grey-eyed guard cuts Tessa's cords. She does not rub her wrists. She sits very straight, with the spear withdrawn from her knees and the knot at the back of her skull plain to see. Another Tally-sworn kneels by Gorrak's pallet with linen and a bottle, and begins to bind the long wound across his ribs. Gorrak lets him. His eye finds yours once and closes.

"And because you were sensible," the Foreman says, "I withdraw my second thoughts. Your sister leaves at first light with a mule, three days' bread and my safe-conduct, as first written. I never say a thing twice that I have ink for." He turns the ledger toward himself to enter the change, and the brown, curled corner of the page comes round under the lamp.

You are not meant to read it, and you do not mean to. But the scorch has eaten the Foreman's careful hand and left the old lines bare, and you have a goat-trader's eye for a column that does not add. Gorrak's line is there, with the date six winters back. Beside it is a small number, one you could carry, struck through with a single neat stroke. Under it, in fresher ink, is the large number that never goes down. The first figure is nearer to nothing than to anything. Two winters ago, by the dates, the captain had all but paid. Someone made him owe it again.

The Foreman's pen does not pause, and he does not look up. The nearest guard is watching your face, so you give him nothing. On the pallet Gorrak is looking at the ceiling, and he has not seen the page.

The Foreman turns the ledger back, caps his ink and folds his soft hands on the beads. The contract lies before you with the term, the surety and the safe-conduct, and the pen is laid beside it. It is a little after eleven-thirty at night, and the room is silent but for the stove. Every spear in it is waiting for you to swear.

You have 1 hit point and a secret about Gorrak that no one else in the room knows you have. What does Korvash do?

Three of the guards freeze where they stand.

She goes down in a heap beside the stove, her one hit point gone, and does not move. You can see the rise and fall of her ribs, but she isn't getting up.

The sixth guard goes for the book. He snatches it off the iron top, beats out the smoulder with his glove and tucks it against his chest. Its pages are scorched half through at the edges, and the cover is blackened, but it is whole. The Foreman has seen it. He heaves your table off his legs. It is a contest of arms that he should not have won, and the roll of his shoulder is a man who has found something he did not know he had. He drags himself upright with the key chain swinging at his belt, his apron torn and his face white, and his voice rises, small and sharp, past the barred door.

"The stair! To me! The stair!"

Boots answer from below, quick and heavy. The three of the other shift are coming up. You have a round, maybe two.

You are at 1 hit point, wired at wrist and ankle to a table that is no longer pinning anything. Tessa lies unconscious by the stove. Gorrak is half-risen against the wall, bound and bleeding. The singed guard is the only man in the room on your side, and he has one hit point. The sixth guard, with the ledger in his arms, is backing toward the Foreman. It is your turn. What does Korvash do?

It is a little before three in the morning.

DM: You drag your feet and let them think you're beaten, and your eyes work the whole way.

The gate-top walk is a narrow stone roof, fifty paces long, with a parapet on the cliff side and the ledger-house at its end. There are six cloaks around you and two more at the door. The ledger-house has two shuttered windows on the walk side, both barred from the inside. The door is oak, banded in iron, and a bar the thickness of your thigh hangs on pegs beside it. At the far end of the roof, past the ledger-house, is a squat stone shed with a slate roof. It's the wash-house, Tessa's old way in. One cloak sits on a stool at its door with his spear across his knees, half asleep. Behind it, you remember, the drain-stair drops into the dark.

At the ledger-house door you plant your heels and say it flatly and loudly enough for the room: you will hear his terms, but you will see Tessa alive and whole before you say a word.

It comes out wrong. It doesn't sound like a condition, it sounds like a plea, and the grey cloak behind you laughs. Inside, the Foreman looks up from his desk, a bandaged shape under the lamp, with the scorched ledger shut in a new iron-bound chest at his elbow and a grey cloak standing over it. He lets the laugh die.

"You are in no position to stipulate, Emberhide. You were in a better one two hours ago, and you spent it badly." His soft voice is cooler than before. "But I'm not a cruel man. Look."

He lifts a finger. Across the room, by the stove, Tessa sits on a stool with a needle in her hand and a roll of linen in her lap. Her left ankle is shackled to the stove-rail. The bruise on her skull has darkened and her lip is cut, but her hands are steady, and she lifts her eyes and meets yours. A tight, quick movement of her chin: *alive*. The Foreman's torn apron is clean now. She has sewn his side.

"There. Whole enough." He folds his hands. "Now hear my terms, because they have changed. You have until the dawn bell. At the bell, you swear, in front of witnesses, to the contract as I first drafted it, with no clauses struck. If you do not, my rider goes to the Border Stones, and Captain Vael and the cripple hang from the gate beam before the bell is done ringing." He looks at the heavy chains on your wrists. "Think carefully. I would so much rather not."

They chain you to the table leg again. It is the same leg, the same iron ring, and the wire is twisted tighter at your wrists. The cloaks withdraw to the walls. For forty minutes nothing happens, and the stove ticks. Tessa sews bandages she doesn't need, with her eyes down and her mouth shut. The Foreman writes, reads, rings his beads, and does not look at you. It is a little before four in the morning. The dawn bell is roughly two hours off.

You are at 1 hit point, with two hours and a roomful of grey cloaks. You've seen the shape of the roof, and you know where the wash-house is. What does Korvash do?

You keep the clan sign tight in your fist all the same, thumb and finger pinched. Nobody saw it, and you had no hand to give it to.

He tilts his head, the first small flicker of interest. And on the first stroke of the dawn bell you fill your lungs and roar through the barred shutters.

The bell does what a bell does. Bronze comes down on the words like a hammer on a pot, and the wind snatches the rest. What carries over the din is a handful of the sentences: the captain's debt, paid down to forty, written up to four hundred, the scorched book, Dreg, Brun. A grey cloak is on you before the second stroke. A gloved fist closes on your jaw and a wad of greasy cloth is forced between your teeth. It is Dreg's voice. It comes again, and then a thud of spears against stone, a few at first, then more, a rhythm picking up under the bell. Out on the walk someone barks an order in the Foreman's grey-cloak voice, and a second voice answers it with a curse, and then a scuffle.

The Foreman sits very still. The beads have stopped moving. He lays his pen aside and speaks without raising his voice. "Bring me the captain. Hang him now, before the bell has finished ringing. And someone, please, close those shutters."

  [game] notebook: updated Brun
DM: You spit the gag and drive your forehead at the nose of the cloak at your shoulder. He sees it coming. The blow glances off his helm and your skull rings, and the sword stays on his belt. You roar anyway, with blood in your mouth. "Dreg! The stair door! Break it!"

He answers with the pommel. Two blows come at once, one wide and one true, and the true one lands on the same ridge of bone as the last. The room tilts and goes out.

You wake slowly, in a grey light, on the floor under the table. The wire at your wrists is cut and the iron is still on. Your head is a drum, and a cold cloth lies across your eyes. It is about ten in the morning, and you have 1 hit point.

The ledger-house is not what you left. The door is barred and braced with the table's twin, and a heavy chest has been dragged against it. Four of the grey cloaks stand at the shutters with crossbows, shuttered slits open a finger wide. The other two have the door. The Foreman sits behind the desk with his hands folded on the beads, very upright. He looks like a man who has not slept and has no intention of saying so. Tessa still sits on her stool by the wall with her wrist in iron, but she is watching the door with her chin lifted.

Through the oak, a voice you know is bellowing in Goblin, a sergeant's voice, cracked from hours of use. "OPEN IT, FOREMAN. We'll read the page. We'll count it aloud. Open it, or we'll burn the walk from under you."

Another voice answers from the stair-head, lighter and thin, a boy's: Brun's. "Captain's alive! The hanging's stopped! He's alive!"

"There," the Foreman says to no one, mildly. "The stair is held by my own men, and the door is held by my own men, and yet here we sit." His eyes find yours, and for the first time you see that he is afraid. It does not make him gentle. "It seems you have made a mess, Emberhide. But a mess can still be tidied. Your voice is the one they heard. You will tell them the page was a lie, that you spoke from fear, and that Greyshaft's books are honest. You will do it at that door, with a knife at your sister's throat and the contract in your hand, and then they will go home. Or you will not."

He nods to the nearest cloak. The cloak sets the point of a blade against the hollow of Tessa's neck.

"Choose," says the Foreman.

You are at 1 hit point, on the floor, with the iron still on your wrists. The Tallies hold the stair and the cloaks hold the room. It's a little before ten in the morning, and your sister has a blade at her throat. What does Korvash do?

"Take the blade off her neck, open the door a hand's width, and let Gorrak read the page aloud before Dreg and the whole stair. If it's honest, I swear your contract at once, and if it isn't, you walk out alive with a safe-conduct, and Greyshaft's miners keep the rest."

  [game] time: 1 minute later: Day 21, 9:57:54 AM
DM: You push up onto one elbow, look at the knife at your sister's throat, and shake your head. You won't lie to those men, for her or for yourself, but you'll offer a tidier road. The blade comes off her neck, the door opens a hand's width, Gorrak reads the page aloud to Dreg and the stair, and if it's honest, you swear the contract on the spot. If it isn't, the Foreman walks out alive with a safe-conduct.

He listens to every word, head tilted, as a clerk listens to a column being read to him by a child. When you finish he lets the silence run a breath too long.

"That is very neat," he says. "And it is exactly the offer a man makes when he knows what is on the page." His eyes slide to the shuttered slit where Dreg's voice is still raging. "If the page were false, Emberhide, you would not be so eager to have it read. I am not going to hand my own throat to a mob with a clean conscience. No."

The cloak at Tessa's neck shifts the blade a finger's width closer. Tessa doesn't flinch, but her breath stops. The two nearest crossbows swing from the shutters to you. The Foreman stands, slowly, and walks to the barred door. He doesn't touch it.

"Sixty breaths," he says, quietly, to the room. "Then the lie is spoken at that door in your voice, or the mender bleeds on my floor, and the cloaks with the bows will do what they are told. I count aloud. I shall start now."

He starts. "One."

You can hear it through the oak: Dreg's voice, the beat of spears, the stair full of men you are fairly sure are on your side. You can see the whole room. Six cloaks, four bows trained at the shutters and you, two blades at the door and at Tessa's throat. The inner door to the bedchamber is barred but unguarded, because the two cloaks that carried the chest are now behind the bows. The lantern burns on the Foreman's desk. Your wrists are in iron, with no wire to hold them now, and your ankles are hobbled.

You are at 1 hit point on a stone floor, thirty breaths from your sister's death. What does Korvash do?

On the last word I swing my chained fists down across the Foreman's lantern, smashing it off the desk so the burning oil spills across his sleeve and the floor between him and the cloaks.

  [game] notebook: updated Break the Foreman's Ledger
DM: You get to your feet slowly, the way a beaten man does, and shuffle to the door in your hobbles. Through the crack you call out in Goblin, flat and loud as a hanged man, that Greyshaft's books are honest. Honest as the knife at your sister's throat. You speak from a cold heart and a full belly. Now come and break this door.

Every word is a lie that Dreg will know for a lie. On the last one you swing your chained fists across the desk. The lantern bursts in a sheet of burning oil, and the Foreman's sleeve goes up with it.

He is quick, and not quick enough. He claws at his arm, and the flame climbs it. A moment later the fire has him, and he dies the way he lived, in small neat motions, without a word. When it is over the room smells of scorched wool and lamp oil, and no one speaks.

Four of the six cloaks drop their blades. The one holding the knife at Tessa's throat lets it clatter to the floor and backs away with his hands open. Two do not. The first puts a bolt past your ear. The second, a flat-faced man by the shutters, puts one through your chest. You go down with an arrow in you and a grey roar in your head, and you feel hands on you, small and quick and steady. Tessa's. She is free of the ring, and she is bent over you with her fingers at your throat, and her voice is in your ear saying *no, you stubborn ox, no.*

Then the door goes. You don't see it, only hear the crash and the roar of Dreg's voice, and the slow ugly sound of spears finding the two who would not yield.

You come back in a quiet room, with sunlight in a long bar across the floor. It is about two in the afternoon, and you have 1 hit point. The shutters are open, the air smells of ash and cold, and the Foreman's body lies under his own grey cloak by the stove. The four cloaks who surrendered kneel in a corner, disarmed, under a pair of Tally spears. The manacles are gone from your wrists, struck off with the Foreman's key. Beside your hand lies Dargan's greataxe with the ember-red sash still knotted on the haft, and the carved horn charm is on its cord across your chest. Someone has put them back where they belong.

Tessa sits against the wall with her knees drawn up and her eyes on you, with the key chain around her neck. She's pale and bruised and very much alive.

Dreg stands at the door, bloodied and grim, with Brun at his shoulder, white-faced and upright. Gorrak Vael sits at the Foreman's desk with the scorched ledger open before him. His burned face is bare, and a bandage is bound across his ribs. He's been reading for a long time. He doesn't look up when you stir.

"It's all here," he says quietly. "Forty. Four hundred. Two winters. Every Tally-sworn in this mountain has a line in this book, and I think half of them are lies." He turns the page. "It is yours to burn, Emberhide, or to keep. Dreg says the men will do whichever you tell them. I have not decided what I think of that." The stove ticks. "And your oath is to me. A year and a day, I think, which I never meant to spend. You may name what you'd have of it."

You are at 1 hit point, but you stand at 300 experience, which is enough to rise to second level whenever you are ready for it. There is a fire in the stove, a book on the desk, and a roomful of people waiting.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 98 ==
PLAYER: I take the book from Gorrak's hands and carry it to the stove myself, so every Tally in the doorway can see me do it. "No one's name stays in the Foreman's hand, Captain, yours or theirs or the children's," I say, and I feed it page by page into the coals.

Then I turn to Gorrak. "As for the year and a day, I name this: free every captive left in Greyshaft and walk them home, and let any Tally-sworn who wishes to come with us do it as an honest man. Then I want one meal at Aunt Veshra's table with my sister, and after that I'll still carry an axe wherever you ask."

  [game] notebook: updated Oath to the Iron Tallies
DM: You take the ledger out of Gorrak's hands and carry it to the stove yourself, slowly, so every Tally in the doorway can see. The pages are brittle at the edges and heavy in the middle, and the book is warm from the stove. You say it plainly: no one's name stays in the Foreman's hand, not the captain's, not the Tallies', not the children's. Then you feed it to the coals, a leaf at a time.

It burns badly at first. Then the good paper catches, and the smell of old ink and scorched leather rolls through the room. Nobody speaks. Dreg's jaw works. Brun has a hand over his mouth. One by one the Tally-sworn in the doorway take their helms off and hold them against their chests. The numbers go up the chimney as smoke, and the last page curls black and falls apart in the fire.

Then you turn to Gorrak and name your year and a day. Every captive left in Greyshaft freed and walked home, and any Tally-sworn who wants to come with you doing it as an honest man. Then one meal at Veshra's table with your sister, and after that you'll still carry an axe wherever he asks.

It's a clumsy way of saying it. You hear the words come out in the wrong order, the last clause first, the Tally-sworn clause blunt as a mallet. Dreg stiffens in the doorway.

Gorrak is quiet for a while. His burned face gives away nothing, but his hand is tight on the desk's edge. When he speaks it is slowly, a man choosing each stone.

"The captives, yes. Every one. I'll take them down with my own hands if I must. I never wanted them in the first place." He draws a breath. "The meal at your aunt's table, yes. I would not keep a man from his sister's bread." Then his eye goes to the doorway, to the helms held against the chests. "The rest, no. These are Tally-sworn, and they swore to me, not to you. I won't have a man walk from my colours because a stranger said he might. If one of them wants to leave, he'll ask me to my face, and I'll hear him, and that is all I will promise." He pauses. "That is more than I have said to any man in six years. Take it or leave it."

Dreg lets out a long breath through his nose. Brun lowers his hand. It isn't what you asked for. But the book is ash, the Foreman is dead, and you have a captain who will not hang you and will not let you go. Tessa has risen from the wall. She comes and stands at your elbow without a word.

It is about twenty past two in the afternoon. The ledger is gone, and you are at 1 hit point and 300 experience, enough to rise to second level.

Before we go on, the level-up choice. Your hit point maximum rises by either the fixed value, which is 9, or a d12 roll plus your Constitution modifier of 2, with the House minimum of 1 per level. You also gain Reckless Attack (advantage on your Strength melee attacks for a turn, but attacks against you have advantage too) and Danger Sense (advantage on Dexterity saves against effects you can see). Do you take the fixed 9 or roll?

PLAYER: Fixed 9, I've had enough bad luck with dice for one chapter. I accept Gorrak's terms: the captives walk home, the meal at Veshra's table comes first, and I'll hear nothing from his men that they haven't said to his face.

Then I turn to Tessa and put a hand on her shoulder. "Let's find Ninna's friends in the Tin Row and the rest of the hands, and start the walk to Harrowmere."

DM: You take the fixed nine, and the strength comes back into your legs. The sheet is yours: second level, Reckless Attack and Danger Sense, and ten hit points of twenty-three. You nod to Gorrak. The captives walk home, the meal at Veshra's table comes first, and you'll hear nothing from his men that they haven't said to his face.

Then you put a hand on Tessa's shoulder. She is shaking a little, and she doesn't pull away. "Let's go and find who's left."

Ninna's friends are no longer in the Tin Row. Ushk's ten spears have long since taken them down the high road. But the shaft still holds thirty-eight children, the row you last saw empty, now crowded back into the gallery since the stair door fell. Past them are sixty-odd miners from the lower galleries: men and women with tin-grey hands and eyes that have forgotten how to look at a door. When Dreg's sergeants strike the last shackle with the Foreman's keys, nobody moves. A boy about the age of the one who had your whisper in the dark stands and stares at his own bare ankle. Then he looks up at you, and the whole row follows.

It takes the rest of the afternoon. Dreg's Tallies carry water and bread up from the barracks. Gorrak walks the galleries himself, his bandaged ribs held stiff, without his mask. The miners stare at the burn on his face. Nobody speaks to him. When he reaches the end he stands for a while looking at the dark mouth of the shaft, then orders the door barred and the keys thrown into the cinder yard. He says nothing about it afterward.

Tally-Pip finds you in the cinder yard as the light goes. He is thinner than you remember, ink to his elbows, and he won't meet your eye until Tessa kneels in front of him. "Where does a ledger-boy go," he asks in Goblin, in a very small voice, "when the ledger's gone?" Tessa looks at you. She doesn't say anything, because she doesn't have to.

It is a little after five in the evening on the second day of the new year's turning. The cloak prisoners are bound in the guardroom. Dreg's spears hold the gates. A cold wind blows ash from the chimney across the yard, and the scorched sun sets over the scree bowl. Behind you, ninety-odd freed hands sit on the cinder in rough rows with bread in their fists.

Gorrak stops beside you, not close. "Dawn," he says. "Dreg will take them down the ore road with forty spears. You and your sister take the front of the column." He looks at the sky. "I would rest, if I were you. I am told it improves a man."

You have a night in Greyshaft, a sister at your side, a goblin boy waiting for your answer, and a long road home. What does Korvash do?

Across the cinder yard a Tally sentry sings the same tune, off-key, to keep awake. Nobody tells him to stop.

You sleep as you haven't in a long time. Nobody comes to wake you with a boot.

It is a little before five on Day 22, with a cold grey in the east. You have rested fully: 23 hit points, both rages, and your breath weapon are back. The column forms in the dark. Ninety-odd freed hands, forty Tally spears, and the long ore road down to the lowlands wait for you at the front. What does Korvash do?

DM: You tie the sash tight, haul Tessa up, and take the front of the column with Gorrak a pace to your left. Dreg grunts and sends his spears to the flanks and rear. The freed hands set the pace, and the slowest walk first.

It is a slow march. The ore road drops out of the scree bowl in long switchbacks, and the grey light turns to a thin winter sun. The children keep close in knots, counting one another's heads the way Ninna taught them. The miners walk with their eyes on the ground, then on the sky, then on the road ahead. Around mid-morning an old woman stops, takes a handful of snow, and holds it to her face. Nobody hurries her. A Tally spearman waits beside her with his helm off, and that's the whole of it.

Pip keeps between you and Tessa as told, with his head down and his ink-stained hands tucked in his sleeves. At noon he asks Tessa, in a whisper, how many flour sacks a table feeds. She says it depends on the table. He nods gravely, as if that were a fair answer.

Gorrak says little all day. Once, on a steep stretch, he stops to look back at the grey mouth of the mountain, then walks on. Near evening he says without looking at you, "Six winters I counted toward a day I thought would come. It did not come as I imagined." He doesn't say more, and you don't press him.

By late afternoon the column reaches a sheltered hollow with a cold spring and a ring of old fire-stones, a day short of the lowlands. Dreg calls the halt. Fires are lit, water is carried, and the children are bedded down in the lee of the rocks. Harrowmere is another day's walk, and you may reach it tomorrow by dusk. It's about five in the afternoon, and you're unhurt and rested.

Brun comes to you across the fire-light, twisting his helm in both hands. He is young, thin-necked, and scared of his own question. "Sir," he says in Goblin. "When we get to the town, what happens to the Tally-sworn who walked out of the mountain? Will they let us in the gates?"

Tessa looks at you, and so does Gorrak. What does Korvash say or do?

I would not have thought of it." He turns his face to the flames, the burn on it livid in the light. "A captain should have."

The evening goes quietly. The children eat and are bedded down. Pip falls asleep with his head on Tessa's knee. She sits with her back against the rock, humming the counting song. Beyond the firelight the road runs down into the dark. Tomorrow it brings you to the Border Stones, and then Harrowmere. It's nearly seven.

DM: You carry your bread across the fire and sit down opposite Gorrak with the axe across your knees. He doesn't look up at once. He turns a stick in the coals.

"What becomes of them," he says. "The Tallies were never an army, Emberhide. They were a debt with spears. Men who owed the Foreman, or whose fathers did, and a captain who kept the count so they would not starve." He breaks the stick. "Without the book, half of them will drift to the high roads and take what they can carry. That is what hungry men with spears do. The other half will ask me what honest pay looks like, and I have never once paid a man in anything but fear."

He is quiet for a time. "Dreg has said a thing I did not like. That a drover's road is only as safe as the men who walk it, and the ore road wants guards. I told him no town would hire the men who burned its steadings." His eye moves to you at last. "Perhaps he is right. I am not asking."

Then you speak of Veshra's table. Gorrak's mouth twists, and for a moment you can't tell if it is pain or something near a smile. "A burned face at the table of a woman whose brother's pass I took. She would feed me and she would be right to hate me." He sets the broken stick down. "I will come to the Stones. I will not promise the table. I will promise to decide there, with my eyes open."

Across the fire Dreg pretends not to listen, and Tessa pretends to be asleep. It's nearly eight. The camp has gone quiet, the children are bedded down, and the sentries walk the ring of the hollow.

What does Korvash do now?

The night passes without alarm. The next day is the long walk down. The switchbacks give way to rolling country, then frost-bitten fields, then the first stone walls and the smoke of lowland chimneys. The freed children begin to run ahead and are called back. A miner kneels and puts his bare hand flat on a wall as if to be sure it's real. Dreg keeps his spears in a tight, quiet line with their helms off, as you told them.

You come to the Border Stones at about five in the afternoon, with the light going gold and thin. The three weathered stones stand where Gorrak once stood to promise you your father's axe. But they are not alone.

The high road is full of people. Ushk stands at the front with his ten spears, helm in hand. Behind him are the herders of Harrowmere, hundreds of them: shawled women, old men on sticks, boys on walls, a woman holding a lamp though it isn't yet dark. A white-haired woman in a clan shawl stands at their head, straight-backed and small. Aunt Veshra. Beside her a weathered, broad-shouldered drover leans on a staff and looks at the hobgoblin column the way a man looks at weather. That must be Hale Orrin. Pell stands at Veshra's elbow with Wren on his hip, and Wren is staring at you with her mouth open.

The road goes quiet. Behind you, forty hobgoblin spears stop walking. Ahead, hundreds of herders wait. Nobody has drawn a weapon, and nobody has spoken.

Tessa's hand finds yours and grips it hard. Pip presses against your leg. Gorrak stands a step behind your left shoulder with his burned face bare.

What does Korvash do?

DM: You lift Dargan's horn charm high where the whole road can see it, then walk forward with Tessa's hand in yours and Pip at your heel. You stop an arm's length from Veshra.

For a moment the old woman says nothing. She looks at the charm, then at your face, and last at the small rust-scaled woman at your side. Her mouth works. Tessa says, in a voice you've never heard from her, "Aunt." Veshra drops her stick. She takes Tessa's face in both hands and looks at the scar, the bruises, the amber eyes. Then she pulls her in so hard that Tessa makes a small sound, and the crowd breaks into the noise of three hundred people trying not to weep. Wren squirms down from Pell's hip and runs at you, and you catch her one-armed against your chest. She smells of woodsmoke and goat.

Over Tessa's shoulder Veshra's eyes find you. "You are late for supper," she says, thick-voiced, "and you are thinner." She wipes her face with her shawl and turns to the road.

Then you say the rest, loudly: the hobgoblins waited at your word with spears sheathed and helms off, the burned man is Gorrak Vael who spared Tessa on the ledge, and every hand should be heard before anyone raises one. Murmurs run through the crowd. A man near the front hisses *butcher*. A woman picks up a stone and holds it without throwing. Hale Orrin lays his staff across the man's chest without looking at him. "Hush," he says, in a voice like gravel. "We'll hear it."

Veshra draws herself up. "On this road, in front of these stones, anyone may speak who came unarmed," she says. "Let the one with the burned face come forward."

Gorrak walks out alone, past you, and stops at the middle stone with his hands open. He doesn't ask for mercy, and he doesn't tell the story of the ledger. He says, plainly, that the Tallies raided this country, that he led them, and that he can't give back what was taken. He says the book that held them is ashes and the children are home. Then he says his men can be guards or bandits, and that he'd rather they were guards. He names Dreg and Brun as men who stood against the Foreman. He asks for no pardon.

No one speaks. Dusk is gathering, the lamp-woman's light has begun to matter, and three hundred faces turn from Gorrak to Hale Orrin, who leans on his staff and chews slowly on nothing.

"A man who'll say that with his mask off," says the drover at last, "has got something to say. I want to hear the rest of it. But I want to hear it from you too, Emberhide. You've sworn to him. Why?"

All of Harrowmere is waiting. What does Korvash say?

You tell it from the beginning: the cairn and the horn goat with the notched crescent, the culvert and Tessa's thin voice humming the counting song, the ledger, the Tin Row, the chute, the ledge. When your words falter, Tessa picks them up. Where she cannot go on, you take her hand. Veshra says nothing through all of it. Her hands lie flat on the table. When you reach your father's name her knuckles go white, and when you reach the stair door she gets up, goes to the hearth, and stands with her back to the room for a while.

When it's done the fire is low and the children are asleep in heaps on the benches. Wren has Pip's head on her lap. Veshra comes back and puts a cup of warm milk in front of you, and another in front of Tessa. "Your father would have said you were loud," she says, her voice rough. "And I would have said he was proud of it."

Later, in the quiet, Tessa leans close. "The cairn," she says. "At the thaw, or before. I want to go with you, Korvash. I've not seen it since." You hear the question under it. She has been to the Goat's Throat on a dozen nights alone, but never with you.

It is almost nine in the evening of Day 23. The Tallies are camped at the Stones, the town meets at the thaw, and your sister is home. You have the night, and the season, and your oath still to carry.

What does Korvash do?

DM: You take the goat's halter in one hand and Tessa's pack strap in the other and set a pace slow enough for talking. The old path climbs out of the lowland fields into scrub and then bare rock, with frost still white in the shadows. For the first hour Tessa says nothing. Then, as the track steepens, she begins.

"You came back for me. That's the part you don't remember. The roof-pole came down in the byre, and the smoke was so thick I couldn't see my own hands. You had gone out and come back in, and a beam hit you, here." She taps her own temple. "You fell on me. I got out from under you and I dragged you by the belt. Out the back, up the goat track, past the kids' pen. I was seven, Korvash. You weighed like a dead ox." She gives a short, odd laugh. "I got you as far as the Weeping Ledge and I held a rag on your head until it stopped. You asked for Father. I said he'd gone to hold the pass."

You walk on a while. The goat's hooves click on the stones. She doesn't say what happened after the ledge, and you don't ask.

At the split boulder, a grey slab cloven by lightning, you stop where the path divides. You took the wrong fork here in the dark once, and you tell her so. She stifles a smile and steps past you without a word, takes the left fork, and glances back. "The right goes to a cliff. You went right. I came back and found you looking at the drop." She says it fondly, and you realize that's the second story in one morning in which she's pulled you back from something.

The climb goes on through noon, with a cold lunch of Veshra's bread and goat on a flat stone out of the wind. The path narrows, then narrows again, and at last it enters the cut between two walls of dark rock, no wider than a cart, the wind howling through it. This is the Goat's Throat.

It's about half past two in the afternoon. Beyond the cut, on a shelf of wind-scoured turf where the path opens out, stands the cairn: a long heap of grey stones mortared with lichen, taller than you remember. Someone has been tending it. A rag of fresh red wool is tied to the topmost stone, and by it lie three small things: a carved horn, a knucklebone, a withered sprig of mountain thyme. A stone circle ringed the base once, and one stone is missing.

The goat has stopped pulling. Tessa has stopped walking, one hand on your sleeve. The wind drops, as winds sometimes do at such places, and the quiet is very large.

What does Korvash do?

DM: You give Tessa the halter and walk the last paces alone. You kneel at the long slab, and the stone is so cold it aches through your palm. You don't shout. For once there's no one to hear you but the wind and the goat.

"Father, I brought her. She's whole, the ledger's ash, and I kept the word I swore with your blood on my hands."

The wind says nothing. But your throat closes, and you stay there with your forehead nearly on the stone for longer than you would admit to anyone. When you stand, you draw Tessa down beside you. Together you wind the halter-rope about the cairn's base stone, so that the young white goat stands tied to the herd's own mark, counted, with one dark ear twitching in the wind.

Tessa lets go of the rope and opens her pack. From the bottom she takes something wrapped in a rag: a smooth grey river stone the size of both fists, with a notched crescent carved into one face. She turns it over in her hands. "It fell out of the ring when I was seven," she says. "I picked it up when we ran. I didn't mean to take it." She looks at the gap in the circle, the one missing stone. "I've carried it ever since. I think I was afraid that if I put it back, it would mean he really was gone."

She sets it in the gap. It fits like a tooth in a jaw.

You stay another hour. You light a stick of incense from your pack, as an acolyte should, and it burns straight in the still air. Tessa hums Dargan's counting-song, all the way to the end this time, and you hum the second half with her. The goat lies down in the lee of the cairn. The afternoon light slants gold across the shelf and the thin white line of the pass below. The thyme and the knucklebone stay where they are. Whoever tended this cairn has not come while you were here, and you don't know who it was.

It is half past three. You're tired, calm, and whole in a way you haven't been since you were nine. Harrowmere is a day's descent, the Tallies are camped at the Stones, and the thaw meeting is weeks away. Evening is coming on up here, with a long night ahead if you stay.

She leads you up a narrow shelf of rock behind the cairn, over a lip of scree, and along a ledge no wider than your two feet. At the end a thin sheet of water falls from the overhang in a curtain, the seep for which the place is named. Tessa ducks under it as if she were stepping through a door, and you follow, soaked at the shoulders and grinning in spite of yourself, the goat bleating complaint behind you.

Behind the water there is a dry hollow the size of a small byre. The floor is level, the air smells of stone and thyme, and a rock bench runs along one wall. On the back wall, at about the height of a seven-year-old, a child has scratched seven goats and a crescent, and beneath them a row of tally-marks. You count them without meaning to. There are eleven. Eleven nights. Tessa looks at them a long time and says nothing.

You notice that the floor has been swept. On the bench stands a clay cup with a sprig of fresh thyme in it, just as there was at the cairn. Tessa sees it too, and the two of you look at each other. "Not me," she says. "I've not been here in years." Whoever it was, they come here, and you've no way to know when. You settle the matter between you without speaking: you'll ask Veshra and Hobb Teyl, and you'll leave the cup where it stands.

You share the last of Veshra's bread and the dried goat in the dry behind the seep, with the white goat folded against the wall, chewing. It's dusk by the time you finish. You lie down on old bracken with your axe by your hand, Tessa's back against yours, and the water singing outside the doorway all night like a counting song. No wolves, no hobgoblins, no ledgers. For the first time in a long while, no one is counting you.

You wake before dawn on Day 25. It's about half past five, the seep is silver in the grey light, and your sister is already awake, sitting at the lip of the ledge with her knees drawn up, looking down at the pass.

The walk down to Harrowmere is a day. The thaw meeting is weeks away, Gorrak's Tallies wait at the Stones, and Aunt Veshra's table has a long bench with room for the lot of you. Ushk's jaw works. He has fought a dozen raids and walked a column of children down a mountain without flinching, and he can't seem to lift his eyes.

"It was me," he says, hoarsely, in Common. "Every season since. At the thaw, at midsummer, at the first frost." He turns the helm over in his hands. "I was a young spearman in the line at the Goat's Throat, the day your father held the pass. I was the first through. He put me down with the flat of his axe, and he stood over me with the edge raised, and I thought I was dead." He swallows. "He said, 'Go home to your mother, boy.' And he turned to the next man."

The fire pops. Nobody at the Stones moves.

"It was Brakk who killed him. From behind, while he was facing the others. I saw it, and I did nothing. I lay in the stones with my head ringing and I did nothing." His voice cracks. "After, when the raid was over, I went up at night and found the body. I gathered what stones I could. Your folk built the true cairn after, but I put the first ones on him." He looks up at last. "And I found the ledge, three days after. A child's tally on the wall. I knew whose. I only wanted it known that your father let me go home. I went home to my mother and she lived four more winters. That is a thing I owe him."

Then she pulls a short knotted cord from her sleeve and ties it round your wrist: three knots, a counting-tie. "You come back and untie it yourself," she says. "Don't make me come up the road after you." She turns away, and her shoulders are stiff, and then she turns back and hugs you hard around the ribs. Then she goes.

Dawn on Day 26 is cold and clear. The ponies stamp at the Stones, steam rising from their nostrils. Ushk sits one with his helm on his saddlebow and his eyes forward. Six of Dreg's best wait in two columns behind him, spears sheathed in leather. Pip stands by Veshra's gate with a blanket round his shoulders, watching you go, and doesn't wave. Wren does enough waving for both of them.

You've the knotted cord on your wrist, a stolen pony under you, and a day and a half of high road to Cinder Ford. It's six in the morning. The mountain is pale at your back, and ahead of you the road runs north and empty.

What does Korvash do?

At the first rest, I'll sit with Ushk and have him tell me everything he remembers of Brakk, his habits, his hiding places, and how he fights, so I can take him without killing him.

DM: You touch the three knots at your wrist, nod once to Veshra and Pip at the gate, and ride out at the head of the column with Hale on your right and Ushk on your left. You tell Hale you'll keep the slowest pony's pace and light no fires after dark, since Brakk knows these roads as well as any of you.

The high road runs north through winter fields and then up into rough country, and the day goes by in cold sun and ponies' breath. Hale does the work of a tracker, leaning from his saddle. At the fork below the Hollin spring he stops, dismounts, and kneels in the frozen mud. "Ten," he says at last. "Heavy boots, one set dragging a left foot. Three days old, maybe four. They've gone north." He is sure of it, and it settles your route.

At midday you rest in the lee of a dry-stone wall. You sit down by Ushk with your bread and ask him to tell you everything he remembers of Brakk. He considers it, chewing slowly. "He fights with a longsword and shield, never the spear. He sits with his back to rock and never to open ground, and he keeps a sentry rota even in his sleep. He doesn't drink on duty. He bullies the weak, and with the strong he goes very quiet." Ushk's mouth twists. "When he's cornered, he doesn't shout. He gets mean and still. That's when to watch him." He thinks a moment more. "He knows the fold-house at Cinder Ford, the ruined one with the half roof, and the ford-stones. If the coin is under them he'll stay close, so he can watch it. He won't leave it for the first soldier that asks."

You turn what you know over in your head the rest of the afternoon. Brakk is no coward. He'll fight, but there's a way to take a man like that without killing him, and it's a matter of where you stand.

By seven the light is gone. You make a cold camp in a dry gully off the road, with the ponies hobbled and no fire. Hale takes the first watch. The six Tally spears lie on the frozen ground without complaint. Ushk sits with his back to a rock, as he must have learned from Brakk, and watches the road.

Cinder Ford is half a day's ride ahead. The stars are out and the frost is deepening. Your wrist is cold under Tessa's knots.

What does Korvash do?

DM: You take the second watch so Hale can sleep and sit with the greataxe across your knees, thumbing Tessa's three knots while the frost deepens. Nothing stirs but the ponies. Near the top, your boot comes down on a slab that rocks, and a stone the size of a loaf goes clattering away down the slope. You freeze with your heart hammering.

Nothing comes of it at first. You crawl the last few yards and look down. Cinder Ford lies below you in the grey light: the shallow black stream, the humped ford-stones, and the ruined fold-house on the near bank with half its roof gone and its back against a wall of rock. You count three sentries, one at the ford-stones and two on the fold-house roof. It looks a thin watch for ten men. You tell yourself that's right, and then you notice you're not sure. The light is poor and your eyes are tired.

A hobgoblin on the roof turns his head toward the ridge. He stands very still. He lifts a hand to his mouth, and a low, flat whistle carries up the slope, two notes, then two again.

Below, the fold-house yard comes alive. Men you did not count rise out of the tumbled wall beside the ford-stones, two more at least. Others come out of the doorway, shields up, swords out, quick and silent. You see a big shape come last, a ring glinting in one ear, a longsword in one hand and a round shield in the other. He doesn't shout. He stands in the yard with his back to the rock, looks up at the ridge, and waits.

Ushk is two hundred paces back with Hale and the spears, hidden in the gully. Brakk knows someone is on the ridge. It's seven in the morning, the sun is just touching the ridge-top, and you are on the skyline alone.

Splint, water, guard, and no one touches him." Dreg nods. Gorrak looks at the barrel. "That is wages, owed to men who bled for it," he says. "It isn't mine and it isn't the town's, not tonight. I will not touch it before the thaw meeting decides." Then, lower: "You brought him back with his heart beating. I would not have asked it of you. I am glad it was done that way."

The nine are led to the Tally fires, where bread and a kettle wait. Ushk goes with them, and nobody stops him. Hale mutters about a hot meal and a cold ale and goes off toward town.

Veshra waits on the road in her shawl, leaning on her stick, a little apart, watching you with her mouth firm. It's seven in the evening, you are home, and tomorrow the hearing can be called. Tonight the long table is laid.

What does Korvash do?

Then I stand and offer her my arm. "Come on, supper's waiting, and I promised Pip I'd eat one of his flour-cakes with a straight face. After that I'll sleep in my old bed for a full night, with the axe by the wall and no one counting me."

"The morning after next, here at the Stones, with the town to hear it. Get up, boy, you'll freeze your knees." You get up, and offer her your arm. She takes it and leans on it a little more than she would admit.

Supper is as warm as the first night, with fewer borrowed bowls. Pip brings the flour-cake on a board with great ceremony. It is round, golden and almost even, and you eat it entire with a perfectly straight face. "It's good," you say, and mean it. Pip's whole face goes pink to the ears. Wren demands a second one for herself and eats it with her fist. Tessa sits at your elbow with the knotted cord wound around her fingers and says nothing at all, which is the best thing she could say.

At nine you climb to your old bed under the rafters, with the greataxe against the wall and Dargan's sash on the haft. Nobody comes with a boot. Nobody counts you. You sleep through the night and wake rested, with your strength and your rage and your breath all back.

It's five in the morning on Day 29. Snow has fallen softly overnight, and the roofs of Harrowmere are white. The hearing is tomorrow. Today is yours, with the town stirring and the Tallies camped at the Stones.

What does Korvash do with the day?

After that I'll walk down to the Stones and spend the afternoon with Ushk and Dreg going over exactly what Ushk will say at the hearing, so the testimony is plain, honest and nothing but the truth.

You lift the cart while Pell's father shims the axle, and you re-lay slates until the leak stops. Pell hands you nails with great seriousness. Pell's father watches Pip with a wary eye. Pip counts the slates aloud, and when he comes up two short, quietly tells Pell's mother, who gives him a heel of cheese. Pell asks you in a rush whether he could carry water to the Tallies' camp, because Wren says Ushk is kind, and she says Wren should know. You say you'll ask Ushk.

Ushk will tell what he saw, no more and no less: Dargan downed him and spared him, and Brakk struck Dargan from behind. You make them say it in order, then again, and then you tell them to stop, because a rehearsed story is worse than a plain one. Ushk nods. "Plain," he says. "I only know plain."

Gorrak sits a little way off, listening, and doesn't intrude. By four in the afternoon the light is going. Brakk lies under guard in a lean-to with his arm splinted, awake and silent.

The hearing is at dawn tomorrow. The town will gather at the Stones, Veshra will ask the question, and the Tallies' fate will hang on how the town hears it. Your evening is your own.
