DM: You take a waterskin and a heel of bread to the lean-to. The guards let you in without comment. Brakk lies on a straw pallet with his splinted arm across his chest, awake, and he watches you as you check the bindings. They are sound, and Ushk's hand is in them. You hold the skin to his mouth and he drinks. You put the bread by his good hand.

You tell him that tomorrow Veshra asks the question, that he can answer truly or lie again in front of the town, and that either way you will see him heard fairly. You don't gloat. He looks at you for a long moment and says, "Fair. You're Dargan's son after all." His tone is flat. You turn it over and can't tell whether it is respect or mockery. Whatever he is thinking, he keeps it, and the lean-to closes behind you on his silence.

You walk back to the fire and sit down by Gorrak. You ask what he wants to say at the hearing, and tell him you'll stand beside him when he says it, and that Hale will want to hear his plan for the ore road before he decides anything.

Gorrak takes his time. He turns the stick in the coals, as he did that night in the hollow. "Three things," he says. "First, that the Tallies raided the lowlands and that I led them. I will say it plain, with no ledger to hide in. Second, that I put myself in the town's hands at the thaw. Judge me as you judge me." He raises his eyes. "Third, that the men who choose to stay hold the ore road in Harrowmere's pay, out of the Foreman's barrel, each man named, each wage written where any may read it. No man owes any other man anything past the day's work." He pauses. "That last is what the mountain never had. I should like to see if a thing can be run that way."

He looks at you sidelong. "Stand beside me. Do not speak for me. If I cannot say it myself, it is not worth saying."

It's six in the evening, with the snow falling again and dark settling over the Stones. Tomorrow at dawn the town gathers.

He taps the pipe out. "I'll tell you this much. What Brakk let slip on the road, I heard it with my own ears, and I'll swear to that in any court. That I can give you tonight." That's all I want."

It is eight in the evening, the snow has stopped, and the hearing is at dawn. The house settles around you, one soft snore at a time.

DM: You squeeze Tessa's hand and take your place a pace behind Gorrak's shoulder, with the greataxe grounded and both hands folded on the haft. You say nothing. You keep your eyes on Veshra and on Brakk's face, and your breath slow.

Veshra waits until the last whisper dies. Then she asks it once, in Common, in a voice like a dry branch. "Did you strike my brother from behind, at the Goat's Throat?"

Brakk does not answer at once. You watch his face, and this time you can read it. It isn't defiance in him, and it isn't fear. He has been counting the faces on the road for a long night: Ushk, who saw it, Hale, who heard him slip on the road, and nine of his own men who heard it too. You see the moment he stops counting.

"Yes," he says, hoarse and flat. A ripple goes through the road. "From behind. Nobody ordered it. The old man was in my way, and I was tired of being held up by a herder with one hand." He looks at Veshra, not at you. "I'd do it again. I'll not pretend otherwise."

Nobody moves. The man whose nephew died at Cinder Ford spits on the frozen ground, but he does not step forward. A woman covers her mouth. Tessa's fingers tighten in yours until it hurts. Behind you, Ushk lets out a long breath, as though he had been holding it for fourteen years.

Veshra closes her eyes for a moment. When she opens them, she is looking at the three stones, not at Brakk. "I thought I would want his blood," she says to the road, evenly. "I find I don't. I want it said, and it has been said." She turns. "Not on this ground, and not by my hand. Let the town judge him at the thaw, as it will judge the rest. Until then he is held, fed and watched." Her voice softens a hair. "Dargan would have said as much. He never did like a messy killing."

A low murmur runs through the herders. Some nod. The grieving man looks at his boots. Brakk lowers his head, and the guards lead him away.

It is a little before seven. The sun is clear of the ridge and the frost is smoking off the stones. Veshra lifts her stick and turns her eyes to the Tallies' line, to the burned face at its head.

"Now," she says. "Captain Vael. You asked to be heard."

Gorrak steps forward. You are a pace behind his shoulder with your mouth shut, as promised. Tessa is at your side, and the whole of Harrowmere is watching. What does Korvash do?

DM: You stay a pace behind Gorrak's shoulder with your hands folded on the haft and your mouth shut. You keep your eyes on the crowd and give Tessa's hand a small squeeze. Your eyes are tired, and you can't be sure of every face in the press of shawls. If a stone is coming, you'll have to meet it by feel.

Gorrak says his three things in his own voice. He does not raise it. He says the Tallies raided the lowlands and that he led them. He says he puts himself in the town's hands at the thaw, to be judged as it judges. Then he says that the men who choose to stay would hold the ore road in Harrowmere's pay, out of the Foreman's barrel, each man named and each wage written where any may read it, with no man owing another anything past the day's work.

When he stops, the road is silent. Then Marn Holt, the grey-faced man with the dead nephew, steps out of the crowd. His voice shakes. "Can you give me the boy back?"

"No," Gorrak says. He doesn't look away. "I cannot."

Marn stands there a long moment. Then he turns his back and walks to the edge of the crowd. He throws nothing. Nobody does, though the road is full of hands holding things. You watch them all, and nothing comes.

Hale Orrin clears his throat and thumps his staff on the frozen road. "Here's what a drover thinks," he says. "I won't have a town of three hundred decide a season's worth in one cold morning. So here is a small thing. Ten of the Tallies, volunteers, named on a list that any can read, walk the high road from the Stones to the drovers' fold through the rest of the winter. Escorts, not guards of anything. Paid in bread and a measure of tin out of that barrel, written openly. No Tally goes into the town. At the thaw, the town judges Brakk, and the captain, and whether there's a season at all." He looks round. "If it goes wrong, the road tells us quick."

Veshra lifts her stick. "I say yes."

There is muttering. Some folk shake their heads, and a few spit. But no one says no loudly, and Marn Holt, with his back still turned, says nothing against it. That is how a town agrees.

Dreg steps forward and names ten from the Tally line. Ushk is the first. Brun is the fourth, and he nearly drops his helm. Gorrak bows his head to the road, stiffly, and you see his hand shake once before he hides it.

It is a little before eight in the morning. The crowd is breaking up into knots, with children running between them. The oath you swore still runs, with eleven months left on it. Tessa is leaning on your arm, and Veshra is coming toward you across the frost.

What does Korvash do?

  [game] time: 6 days 9 hours 55 minutes later: Day 36, 6:00:12 PM
DM: You take an arm of each, Veshra's and Tessa's, and tell them breakfast first. Then you'll need a pack with a mason's line, a maul and a lunch, because you start on Marn Holt's lower wall at first light and mean to finish it by the end of the week. Veshra snorts. "You'll need a stone-hook too," she says, "and your own gloves, for I've seen your hands." On the walk home you ask her about the pony in Marn's pen, and whether Pell might come along to learn dry-stone work. She says the pony's a stout dun gelding who bites, and Pell's mother will say yes if she's asked first. You ask her first.

The week goes by in frost and sweat. Marn's lower wall lies in a long tumble of grey stone at the foot of his field. You haul it out stone by stone, sort it by size, and lay the footing deep. Pell turns out to have an eye. He picks the right stone from the pile a breath before you do, and nods when it fits. On the third day Marn comes out and stands with his arms folded, watching, saying nothing. On the fifth day he brings a pot of broth and sets it on the wall without a word. Pip comes once with flour-cakes, and counts the stones. The count comes out right.

It is finished on the evening of Day 36, a long grey wall as high as your chest, straight as a rule. Marn walks the length of it alone, running his hand along the top. At the far end he stops and puts the last capstone in himself, and stays there with his back to you for some time.

When he comes back, he leads Edric's pony out of the pen, a stout dun gelding named Pebble, with a pale blaze and a wicked eye. He puts the rein in your hand. "He bites," he says. "He'll like you." His voice is rough. "I'd not have the boy's pony stand idle." He glances toward the Stones and away. "I'll hear what that captain of yours does on the road. Not before. But I'll hear it."

It is six in the evening. Dusk is gathering over the steading and the first stars are out. Pell is wiping his hands with a rag, and Pebble is trying to eat your sleeve.

Hoofbeats come hard along the east road. A rider is pounding toward the gate, alone, a thin figure in a Tally cloak. He pulls up in a spray of frost and slides off before the horse stops. It is Brun, white-faced and out of breath, with his helm in his hand.

"Emberhide," he gasps in Goblin. "The escort. Ushk sent me. We found tracks near the fold-house at Cinder Ford, unshod, a dozen men, grey cloaks. And a drover swears he's seen a lantern on the ridge above the Goat's Throat three nights running." He gulps a breath. "Ushk says it's near your father's cairn."

What does Korvash do?

I vault onto the dun and turn him toward the Goat's Throat. "Brun, ride with me and tell me everything Ushk saw, the number, the weapons, which way they came, and keep your helm on."

DM: You take Pebble's rein and swing the greataxe onto your back. You clap Brun on the shoulder and tell him it's the Foreman's leftovers, and they won't touch your father's cairn. You send Pell running for the town with orders: tell Tessa and Veshra where you've gone, and ask Gorrak to send Dreg and six spears up the goat-path behind you. Pell is gone before you finish, small and fast on the frozen road.

You vault onto the dun. He lays his ears flat and tries to bite your knee, and you tell him in Draconic to mind his manners. He doesn't, but he goes. Brun falls in at your side, and as you ride he tells it plainly in Goblin, helm on as you asked. There are about a dozen of them, he says. Grey cloaks, crossbows and short swords, no shields, unshod, and their tracks come down from the north along the old herd track, the Greyshaft side. They have been seen with a lantern on the ridge above the Goat's Throat for three nights. Ushk thinks they are survivors who ran when the mountain fell, men who served the Foreman and have nowhere to go. He doesn't know what they want at the cairn. He doesn't think they know about the hearing.

You ride up through the dark, past the lowland fields and the scrub, onto bare rock. Pebble stumbles once on the frozen track and you nearly lose him, but he picks his feet up and carries on, grumbling. The stars are very bright. The cold bites your face. By half past eleven you reach the split boulder, and you are a short climb below the Goat's Throat, with the wind howling through the cut above.

Brun reins in and points without a word. High on the ridge above the pass, small and steady, a lantern burns. A second light answers it from further along, a dull orange spark. Neither is moving. You can see no figures.

It's half past eleven at night, you're a hundred paces from the cut, and Dreg's spears and Tessa are hours behind. What does Korvash do?

If they've harmed the cairn or the Weeping Ledge, I'll hold my fury until I can see them, because I won't charge twelve crossbows in the dark.

  [game] notebook: created an entry for Skarn; added 3 relationships to Skarn
  [game] time: 40 minutes later: Day 37, 12:10:12 AM
DM: You dismount, put Pebble's rein in Brun's hand, and whisper that he's to stay below the boulder with the ponies and bring Dreg's spears up the instant they come. He nods, white-lipped. Then you go up the old goat-path on foot, bent low, with the greataxe slung so it won't scrape. You tell yourself that if they've harmed the cairn or the ledge, you'll hold the fury until you can see them. You will not charge twelve crossbows in the dark.

The path is your father's, and your feet remember it before your head does. The wind howls in the cut and covers the small noises. You put your hands where the rock is dry, you stay in the shadow of the scree, and nobody turns. By midnight you are lying flat on the lip of the shelf with your chin on cold stone, and the cairn is thirty paces away.

You count them. There are twelve grey cloaks, not a head more. They've made camp in the lee of the cut with a hidden fire sunk in a pit, so that only the glow on the rock gives it away. Crossbows are stacked in a row against the wall, and short swords lie by the bedrolls. Two lookouts stand at the edge of the shelf, facing south, toward the road. A third sits on the ridge above with the lantern, trimming it at intervals. That is the signal light. The orange spark further along is the fire, reflected.

The cairn is untouched. The red wool still flutters on the top stone, and the carved horn and the thyme lie where Ushk put them. The new stone you saw Tessa set in the ring is in its place. They've pitched their camp a decent way from it, as even grey cloaks will from a grave.

Beside the cairn, on the frozen turf, a figure sits bound hand and foot, gagged, in a Tally cloak. It's one of Ushk's escort, a young hobgoblin you half remember. A thin, flat-faced hobgoblin in a grey cloak squats before him, turning a knife idly in the firelight. You know that face. He rapped the deep-shaft door with his spear the night you whispered to Brun. He has the ease of a man waiting for a fish to take the hook.

"He'll come," you hear him say, in Goblin. "The old man's son. He's the sort who runs toward a cairn." He taps the bound man's knee with the flat of the knife. "And when he does, we put a bolt in him, and then we take him down the mountain in a sack."

It's ten past midnight. The twelve have the shelf, and the bound man is bait. You are thirty paces off in the dark with a greataxe, a rage and a breath weapon. Dreg and Tessa are hours behind.

What does Korvash do?

Lay down your crossbows and walk to Harrowmere in the morning, and not one of you will be touched."

While their heads turn to the dark, I crawl a few paces sideways along the lip toward the bound Tally, so that if they bolt or shoot, I can be at him with the axe before the flat-faced one's knife moves.

  [game] notebook: updated Skarn
  [game] time: 2 minutes later: Day 37, 12:12:12 AM
DM: You cup your hands and let the voice roll off the rock so that it seems to come from everywhere at once. Dreg and forty Tally spears are on the goat-path below. The Foreman is dead, his ledger is ash, and nobody left in Greyshaft is paying a grey cloak. Lay down the crossbows and walk to Harrowmere in the morning, and not one of them will be touched.

It lands. You hear it land. The echo comes back off the cut in pieces, *forty*, *ash*, *touched*, and the shelf goes quiet. Heads turn toward the dark below. A cloak by the fire says, "Forty?" in a thin voice, and two crossbows dip. Five or six of them shuffle, looking at each other and at the stack of weapons, and one lets his bow slide to the turf.

Skarn doesn't. He stays on his heels with the knife on the bound man's knee, and his eyes narrow slowly. "Forty," he says, flat. "The captain has twenty and half of them sworn to me, or to the dead man's coin. Don't be fools. Forty spears don't climb a goat-path in silence."

You crawl sideways along the lip toward the bound Tally while their heads are turned. It is the right idea and the wrong moment. Your boot comes down on a slab of frozen shale. It cracks and slides away with a noise like a dropped plate, and a lookout at the edge of the shelf whirls and points. "There! On the lip!"

Skarn is up in one motion. He hauls the bound Tally to his feet by the collar and sets the knife to his throat, and the gag muffles a sound from the young hobgoblin that is half a whimper. Skarn's voice rings across the shelf, sharp and pleased. "Alone, then! Stand up, old man's son, and drop that axe. Or the boy goes over the edge, and then you."

Six crossbows have come up and are trained on the lip where you lie. The rest are scrambling for theirs. The wind howls in the cut. Below, in the dark, Dreg and Tessa are still hours away. Skarn's knife is under the bound man's chin, thirty paces off.

It is a few minutes past midnight. What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 138 ==
PLAYER: I stand slowly, hands open, and drop the greataxe from my shoulder into my right hand as if to toss it aside, then call across the shelf in Goblin, calm and loud, "It's me you want, Skarn, so let the boy go, and I walk to you and give you the axe myself."

While they watch my hands, I rage as quietly as I can and take one step at a time toward the cairn, keeping the bound Tally between me and the nearest crossbows. If Skarn so much as nicks him, I charge, hit Skarn first, and breathe fire across the six bows.

DM: You stand slowly, hands open, and let the greataxe drop from your shoulder into your right hand as if you meant to toss it aside. In Goblin, calm and loud, you tell Skarn it's you he wants: let the boy go, and you will walk to him and hand him the axe yourself.

The shelf holds its breath. Six crossbows follow you up from the lip. Skarn tilts his head, and the knife doesn't move from the young Tally's throat. For a long moment you can see him weighing it, a man who has waited three nights for this and cannot quite believe it has come so easily.

You take the first step. Then another, slow and open-handed, the axe hanging loose at your side. Under your ribs, quietly, you let the rage come up. It rises hot and steady behind your teeth, and you keep your face as empty as a beaten man's, and your shoulders slack. You angle your steps so that the bound Tally stays between you and the nearest bows.

Skarn's gaze sharpens, then slides to the axe. He wants it too much to look at your face. "That's it," he says softly, almost kindly. "Slow. The axe on the ground, ten paces out, and then on your knees. The Foreman's successor wants a whole hand." A tight smile. "Not a dead one."

Five of the grey cloaks have not raised their bows. They stand with the weapons dangling and their eyes on you and on their sergeant, and they are not sure. The sixth, the one who spotted you, keeps his crossbow level and cannot stop it shaking.

You are at twelve paces from Skarn now, on open frozen turf, with the cairn at your right and the cut's black mouth behind him. The rage is lit in you. Your breath weapon waits in your chest. The bound Tally's eyes are wide and fixed on yours over the gag.

It's thirteen minutes past midnight. Skarn is waiting for you to put down the axe. What does Korvash do?

If the bows swing on me, I breathe a cone of fire across the nearest six and keep the bound man behind my bulk. "Down, lad! Roll away from the edge!"

DM: The axe goes down from your fingers and you catch the haft on your boot-toe, flick it up into both hands, and charge. You cross the last frozen turf roaring, with the rage pounding in your ears, and swing hard at Skarn with nothing held back.

Skarn is quicker than you hoped. He hauls the bound Tally in front of him and ducks his head, and the blade passes a finger's width from his ear and bites the frozen ground. The knife stays at the young hobgoblin's throat. You have given up your guard to swing, and every one of them sees it.

Skarn's longsword flashes out from behind his hostage and opens a cut along your ribs. Then the crossbows go off. The first volley comes while you are still lunging, and bolts hammer into your shoulder and thigh, though the rage turns the worst of them aside. The second comes when you cannot turn. You count the thumps, six, seven, and then the cold of the turf is against your cheek and the roaring in your ears is only your own blood. Above you the stars wheel slowly. The rage is gone out of you like a lamp blown out.

You can still hear. Three of the grey cloaks have dropped their bows and are backing away with their hands up, and one is being sick on the turf. Skarn is laughing, quietly. "Get the rope," he says. "Gently. He's no good to anyone dead." Somewhere a young voice whimpers through a gag.

You are at 0 hit points, unconscious, at your father's cairn. You have made one death saving throw, a success. Dreg's six spears and Tessa are still hours below on the goat-path, and Brun waits with the ponies at the split boulder. It is a quarter past midnight.

You are unconscious, so there is no choice for you to make this turn. Tell me when you are ready, and I'll roll the next death save and see who reaches you first.

DM: Rough hands roll you over, and a rope bites your wrists. That is the first thing you know. The second is the cold, and the third is a single point of light in the dark, which is the flicker of a hidden fire on rock.

You come back slowly. The stars have wheeled and the wind has dropped in the cut. It is a little after three in the morning, and you are alive at 1 hit point. Someone has stopped the bleeding. A strip of grey wool is bound tight around your ribs, and bolts have been cut from your thigh and shoulder and the wounds packed with moss. Whoever did it was rough, but they did it.

You are propped against the foot of your father's cairn with your wrists tied behind you and a cord at your ankles. The greataxe is gone, and so is Dargan's sash. Skarn has them. He sits across the fire from you with the axe across his knees, running one thumb along the sash as if weighing what it might fetch. The horn charm is still under your shirt, against your chest. Nobody has looked there.

Tarn, the young Tally, is tied a few paces off, awake, staring at you over the gag. Eleven grey cloaks sit around the fire. Three of them sit apart, away from their bows. They are the three who dropped them, and no one has looked at them since.

Skarn sees your eyes open. "Ah," he says pleasantly. "There you are." He rises and walks over. "Be easy. You're worth more whole than not. I was a very junior man in the Foreman's house, and now I am the senior one left. I mean to take a hand back to Greyshaft that can be shown to the others." He crouches. "A great red hand, with a famous name. It will remind them how the world is arranged." He glances at the sky. "We leave before dawn. The sergeant's people on the goat-path will find an empty cairn, and they can weep over it."

He doesn't know that Dreg and Tessa are close. You don't know it either, only that they were coming. The fire ticks, the wind rises again, and the three wavering cloaks watch their sergeant's back.

You are at 1 hit point and bound at the cairn, and you have an hour before the grey cloaks move. What does Korvash do?

- **Free traits:** Darkvision, Menacing, Relentless Endurance and Savage Attacks. Menacing gives Intimidation, and the half-orc package also gives Insight and Religion. Proficiencies are all armor, shields, simple and martial weapons, and STR and CON saves.
- **Two fighter skills** from Acrobatics, Animal Handling, Athletics, History, Insight, Intimidation, Perception and Survival. Athletics and Perception suit a caravan guard.
- **Fighting style:** Archery, Defense, Dueling, Great Weapon Fighting, Protection or Two-Weapon Fighting. Protection or Defense fits her loyalty.
- **Gear:** chain mail or leather armor with a longbow, a martial weapon with a shield or two martial weapons, a light crossbow or two handaxes, and a dungeoneer's or explorer's pack.
- **Background:** the Acolyte background, which gives Shelter of the Faithful, two extra languages, a holy symbol and a prayer book or wheel. Her Orc and Common are already covered.

DM: You stop walking and look hard at Brun. You tell him he was right to say it now and not never. Skarn spoke of a successor who wants a whole hand, so someone is still giving orders. Skarn is in a lean-to a few streets away.

You take Pebble to Marn's steading first. Marn is already at the gate, with his back to the new wall and his hand on the top stone. He takes the halter without a word, runs a thumb down the blaze, and leads the dun into the empty pen. He doesn't look back, but he leaves the gate open.

By two o'clock you are at the Stones, with Brun at your shoulder. You give Gorrak and Ushk the story, and Gorrak listens with his scarred face still. He agrees to the questioning, with one condition. "Ushk stands in the room," he says. "No blade drawn, no hand raised. The town will ask how it was done, and I will not have it said it was done badly."

The lean-to is dim and smells of smoke and burned hair. Skarn sits against the wall with his wrists bound before him and his face blistered pink, the beard gone. He looks up at the half-orc who has walked in, and his eyes are quick and wary. You ask him plainly, as you said you would: who sat above the boulder on a lamp-less horse, and what are they waiting for?

You don't raise your voice. You stand with your shield arm folded across your chest and let him look at the size of you, and at Ushk by the door. Skarn's tongue touches his cracked lip. "Tolk," he says at last, hoarse. "Deputy to the Foreman. He has the lower galleries and the second ledger and about twenty cloaks. The rider is his man. He watches, and reports." He gives a thin laugh that hurts him. "The lantern on the ridge was the signal. I lit it for three nights. If the lantern isn't lit by dawn on Day 40, Tolk brings the twenty down the high road himself. He wants the red one's hand. He'll settle for the town."

Ushk draws a slow breath at the door. Skarn leans his head against the planks and shuts his eyes. He has nothing else to say.

It is two in the afternoon on Day 38. Dawn on Day 40 is a day and a half off. Gorrak waits outside, and the first watch is at dawn tomorrow.

What does Grunna do?

I will not have Harrowmere's children on the high road for this." Hale taps his staff on the frost. "Then not the town," he says. Veshra's mouth thins, but she nods.

You read it as true. He is afraid, tired, and no longer sure which side is worse. He doesn't lie about the lantern, although he looks away when you ask what he'd do in your place.

It is five in the evening on Day 38. The lantern is to be lit at dusk tomorrow, and again on Day 40, and the switchback is to be held. Tessa is waiting for you at Veshra's table, and the first watch starts at dawn.

DM: You eat what Tessa puts in front of you, hot barley and a heel of goat cheese, and tell her the plan straight. Fifteen drovers with slings, the switchback held, the lantern lit at dusk tomorrow. Then you ask her where Korvash's father would have put the slingers, and which stones are loose enough to roll.

She is quiet for a moment, winding the knotted cord at her wrist, and then she is on her feet drawing in the spilled barley with a fingertip. "The Weeping Ledge," she says. "It sits straight above the switchback, behind a water seep, and you'd walk past it a hundred times and never see it. Fifteen could lie there. The goat-path to it starts on the cairn side, behind the third stone." She sketches the bend. "Below it, on the outer bend, the shale shelf has slabs that aren't set. Father levered them down on raiders when I was small. Put two people at the bar, and a file coming up that bend has nowhere to go." She looks up. "Korvash wouldn't have thought of the slabs. He'd have charged."

You laugh, and it comes out softer than you meant. Veshra, in her chair, says nothing, but she pushes the cheese toward you.

You turn in early with your sword and shield by the bed. The house settles, with Pip and Wren snoring on the bench, and you sleep without dreams. Tessa wakes you with a hand on your shoulder in the grey dark. "Dreg's at the door," she says. "He says the dawn watch won't walk itself."

DM: You pull on your chain mail, buckle the shield to your arm, and take a heel of bread from Veshra's table as you go out into the dark. At the Stones, Ushk meets you with a grunt and you clap his shoulder. You take the tally-strip from Brun and read the day's names aloud so every Tally on the line knows you mean to check them. Nobody argues. One of the older spears mutters something about a lowlander with a list, and Ushk tells him to carry water.

The watch goes up the high road with Ushk on your left and Brun on your right. The sun climbs over the ridge as you go. You look at every bend and rock as you pass it, as you were taught on caravan roads. At the switchback below the cut you stop and work it through with a stub of chalk. You mark the loose slabs on the shale shelf of the outer bend, where two people at a bar could drop a file's worth of stone. You mark the Weeping Ledge above, where fifteen slingers could lie behind the seep, and the single narrow stretch where a file of twenty must climb one at a time. Ushk watches you work. "Dargan would have done this," he says. "Different chalk."

It is a long, cold, steady morning, and the road is quiet. You keep your eyes open and give each rock a hard look, but you can't shake a small unease that you have missed something. It is the kind a guard learns to feel and can't always name.

By eleven in the morning you are back at the Stones with the day's names checked and a ring of chalk marks drawn on the shelf. The lantern is to be lit at dusk, in about six hours.

What does Grunna do?

It takes the better part of three hours. Ushk reads the ground like a man who has hunted in these hills, and it is his hand on your arm that stops you, with your own eyes finding the place a moment later. Below an overhang on the east ridge, a flat stone looks straight down on the cut and the cairn. On it is a scraped-out fire-pit gone cold, and beside it a shuttered lamp with the shutter propped open. The unshod bootprints are in the frost, some two days old and some fresh from last night. Wedged in a crack by the stone is a tin token stamped with a small raised hand, fingers spread, as if counting.

Ushk turns it over. "Tolk's," he says. "The Foreman's deputies carry these." He frowns. "The fresh prints go the other way too." He points. They cross the ridge and drop toward the cairn-side goat-path, and they stop behind the third stone. The watcher has been to the Weeping Ledge. He knows it is there, and may well know that it can hide fifteen.

He isn't here now. It is two in the afternoon on Day 39. The drovers come at nightfall and the lantern is to be lit at dusk. The cut below you is empty, the cairn small and red-wooled in the sun.

At nightfall I'll lie in wait with Ushk on his own flat stone, shield low, and take him alive when he comes back for the lamp, so Tolk sees the answering light on time and walks into the slabs.

Ushk has already slipped off to the right. For a heartbeat the dusk is perfectly silent.

Then a shape rises out of the rocks forty feet downslope, a lean figure in a grey cloak with a short sword in one fist. He doesn't answer. He looks at you, at the dark where Ushk went, and at the lamp-less stone. Then he plants a foot and runs.

Ushk lunges to cut him off, and the watcher ducks under his arm. Ushk's boot skids on frost and he goes to one knee with a curse in Goblin. By the time he is up, the watcher is already thirty paces down the east slope, bounding over broken rock toward the herd track. He doesn't look back.

You could run after him. The slope is steep and the light almost gone, and you are in chain mail with a shield. In your pack is a light crossbow, but it's unstrung and unloaded. By the time you were ready, he'd be a shadow among shadows. He has no lamp now, and nothing to answer the cairn signal with. Tolk will know something is wrong long before dawn on Day 40.

DM: You raise your fist and keep it up, and you whisper to Gorrak that no one moves until the file is strung out on the narrow. You tell Dreg to send two spears quietly to meet the four heading for the Weeping Ledge, since they will find it empty and then try to flank. Dreg nods and goes.

The first torch reaches the tight bend. Sixteen cloaks are strung out behind it in the narrow cut, torches bobbing, crossbows slung low. They are looking at the lantern, not at the rock. You drop your fist and roar, "Slabs, now! Slingers, loose!"

The bars go in. Four slabs peel off the shale shelf with a long grinding groan. Two of them miss, thumping into the road. The other two clip a cloak apiece and send them sprawling in a tangle of cloaks and torches. A heartbeat later the slings sing from the overhang, fifteen at once. Four stones find a man, with sharp cracks against iron and bone. Nobody screams. The cloaks are too surprised to shout, and not one has a weapon up.

You step into the road with the shield high and Gorrak a pace behind your shoulder. Sixteen grey cloaks are bunched below you on the bend. Several are hurt, torches scatter on the frost, and the column's head is stalled, with the file pressed up behind.

This is your turn, and the first round is yours. The cloaks are surprised and cannot act until it ends. A word on the tracker: it shows your slot under Korvash's name, since I can't give Grunna her own entry. Treat that as you, at initiative 10.

DM: You call it out in Common and then in Goblin, steady and loud: they are caught on the narrow, so they should lay down their crossbows and walk to Harrowmere at dawn. No more of them need to be crushed or stoned.

For a heartbeat nothing moves. Four crossbows hit the frost, then three more, and a voice in the back cries out in Goblin for the others to stop. You hold your sword low, readied, to cut down the first man who raises a blade.

The first to raise one is the cloak at the head of the file. As his crossbow comes level, your sword whips out in answer and rings off the iron of his helm. It is not a clean hit, and the bolt he looses finds your shield-arm and bites a hand's width under the rim. It stings, no more.

Then the slings sing again. Five stones crack into the file, and four grey cloaks drop where they stand and do not get up. That is the end of the stomach for it. Seven more throw down their bows and kneel in the road with their hands on their heads. Your shield-rim and the choke have held.

One man hasn't knelt. He stands in the middle of the bend with a crossbow in one hand and a tin token on a cord at his throat, stamped with a counting-hand, the twin of the one in your pocket. His eyes go from you to the overhang, to the road behind him, and to the stones. Then he levels the bow at you and pulls the string. The bolt skitters off your shield.

That is Tolk. He is cornered at the head of the column, between you and the road he can no longer run. It is your turn, in the second round, and Gorrak waits at your shoulder.

What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 166 ==
PLAYER: I close the ten paces fast with the shield high, bash Tolk's crossbow wide with the rim before he can reload, and put the point of my longsword at his throat. "That's enough, Tolk. You're cornered, your twenty are in the dust, and the Foreman is dead, so lower it and live."

If he drops the bow, I take the token from his neck with my free hand and hold him for Gorrak and Veshra. If he swings a blade, I strike his sword-arm and disarm him, because I want him alive to stand at the thaw.

DM: You close the ten paces fast with the shield high. Your sword darts for his bow-arm and the point of his throat, but Tolk twists behind the iron of his own shield and the blade glances off his pauldron. You are close enough to smell the smoke on his cloak. He drops the crossbow and tears a short sword from his belt, and swings it in a hard flat arc at your ribs. Your shield takes it, and the jar goes up your arm to the shoulder.

He is flat-faced, thin, and desperate, with a hobgoblin's quiet. The tin token on its cord swings at his throat. He isn't looking at you but over your shoulder, at Gorrak and the Tallies and the kneeling cloaks.

The road behind him is full of his own men with their hands on their heads. Nobody is coming to help him. The slingers wait above, silent. Gorrak stands one pace behind your shoulder, his hand on his rod, but he hasn't drawn.

It is the third round, and your turn. Tolk is one step away, shield up, his sword hanging low. What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 167 ==
PLAYER: I step in under his guard and drive my shield-rim into his sword-arm to knock the blade wide, then hook the haft of my longsword behind his knee and take him to the frost in a heap. "That's the lesson, Tolk, down you go. I told you I wanted you standing at the thaw, but I never promised how."

Once he's down with my boot on his sword-arm, I rip the tin token off its cord, hold it up for Gorrak and the kneeling cloaks to see, and say loudly: "The Foreman's deputy, taken alive. Nobody else needs to die today."

  [game] notebook: created an entry for The Second Ledger; added 3 relationships to The Second Ledger
DM: You step in under his guard and drive the rim of your shield into his sword-arm, and the blade swings wide. You hook your foot behind his knee and heave. Tolk goes down in the frost with a grunt, flat on his back, with your boot on his sword-wrist before he can find his breath. His shield rattles away across the stones.

You tear the tin token off its cord and hold it up so that Gorrak, the Tallies and the kneeling cloaks can all see the little raised hand. "The Foreman's deputy," you say, loud and level, "taken alive. Nobody else needs to die today."

No one answers. Then, down the file, one kneeling cloak lets out a long shuddering breath, and another puts his face in his hands. Ushk is already beside you with a length of cord. Gorrak lowers his hand from his rod and gives you one short, slow nod.

Tolk lies still under your boot. He doesn't struggle and he doesn't curse. He looks up at the pale sky with flat, tired eyes. "You'll want the second ledger," he says, hoarse, in Common. "It's in the lower galleries, behind the tin-scales. It isn't a ledger of children. It lists the names of the people in Harrowmere who sold word of the Goat's Throat and the herds to the Foreman, back when he was a man with a purse." His mouth twists. "Someone sold your pass, half-orc. Someone who sat at a table in your town and ate bread with your uncle."

Ushk goes quiet beside you. Up on the overhang a drover's sling slips from his hand and clatters on the rock.

It is a quarter past six on Day 40. The switchback is held. Four cloaks lie dead, eleven kneel in the road, and Tolk is bound. The sun is coming up over the cut.

What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 168 ==
PLAYER: I keep my boot on his wrist and my voice low, so the kneeling cloaks can't hear. "That stays between us, Tolk. Gorrak, Ushk and I take you and that ledger to Veshra and Hale in private, and not a word reaches the town until we know whose names are in it."

I look at Ushk and Gorrak. "Bind the eleven, bury the four, tend the hurt, and I'll go with Dreg into the lower galleries for the book."

DM: Who killed Marn's nephew Edric at Cinder Ford, and does the second ledger say?

  [game] notebook: updated The Second Ledger
  [game] time: 3 hours later: Day 40, 9:15 AM
DM: You keep your boot on his wrist and your voice low, so the kneeling cloaks can't hear. The ledger stays between you, Gorrak and Ushk until you can take it to Veshra and Hale in private. Not a word reaches the town until you know whose names are in it.

Tolk doesn't argue. He studies you for a long moment, the flat-faced man who was ready to die, and nods once. "A private room," he says. "I'll say the name there. Not on a road with eleven men listening."

Gorrak and Ushk do as you ask without a word. The eleven are bound loosely, the four dead are laid under cloaks to be buried at the foot of the cairn, and the drovers climb down off the overhang to tend the hurt with moss and cloth. By a quarter past nine the road is clear and the sun is high over the cut.

Now for what you asked me, as the DM. Marn's brother's boy Edric, fourteen, was struck down from behind at Cinder Ford by Brakk's own spear, when he would not let go of the herd rope. Ushk heard it from the men of that night and has never told it. The second ledger won't name the killer. But it holds a line, "Cinder Ford, first frost, Holt crossing: one measure of tin," paid to a seller in Harrowmere, and that seller gave the date of the Holts' crossing to the Foreman. Tolk knows the name, and he is keeping it for the room with Veshra and Hale.

So Edric's cousin has two things to carry to Marn. You can't tell him yet, because the name isn't safe. But the book is, in the lower galleries of Greyshaft, and it is lightly held now that Tolk's column is taken. Dreg waits at your elbow with his tally-strip and two spears.

It is a quarter past nine on Day 40. What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 169 ==
PLAYER: I wipe my blade, sheathe it, and tell Gorrak, "Veshra's table first. Tolk gives her and Hale the name in private, then Dreg and I take two spears to the lower galleries for that book before anyone can burn it."

I put a hand on Ushk's arm as he binds Tolk. "Not a word to Marn until we know the name, Sergeant, because he's buried one boy already and I won't hand him a rumor when I can hand him the truth."

  [game] notebook: updated The Second Ledger
  [game] time: 3 hours 15 minutes later: Day 40, 12:30 PM
DM: You wipe your blade, sheathe it, and tell Gorrak that Veshra's table comes first. Tolk will give her and Hale the name in private. Then you and Dreg will take two spears to the lower galleries for the book before anyone can burn it. You put a hand on Ushk's arm where he binds Tolk, and tell him not a word to Marn until you know the name. He has buried one boy already, and you won't hand him a rumour when you can hand him the truth. Ushk stops. "Not a word," he says. "I swear it on the blanket I gave the little one."

The scene at Veshra's table is short and quiet. Tolk sits bound on a stool in the back room with a spear-guard at the door. Veshra stands with her stick, Hale leans on the wall with his arms folded, and you stand by the door. Tolk looks at the three of you, and then at the floor. "Dessa Quill," he says. "The keeper of the drovers' tavern. She heard the herd-calendars over the ale, who crossed where and when, and she sold them to the Foreman's men for tin. Years of it. The Holt crossing at the first frost was hers."

Hale's face goes grey, because he drinks in that room. Veshra says nothing for a long moment, then she taps her stick once on the boards. "Not a word," she says. "Not to Marn, not to the town, not to Dessa. We have the word of a bound enemy, and I won't hang a woman on that." Her eyes find you. "Bring me the book, girl."

It is half past twelve on Day 40. You stand at the edge of the Stones with your pack, shield and sword, and Dreg and two spears beside you. The high road runs north toward Greyshaft, white with frost, and the lower galleries lie a day and more away.

What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 170 ==
PLAYER: I shoulder my pack and say to Dreg, "Two spears is too few for a mountain full of grey cloaks, so take four, and we move fast and quiet on the high road, no fires after dark."

Before we go, I ask Veshra for one favor. "Keep Dessa Quill's tavern in sight these three days, Aunt, and tell Hale to drink there as usual and say nothing. If she runs, we'll know who sold the Holts' crossing."

DM: Veshra's mouth thins, then she nods. She will have the tavern watched these three days, and she'll tell Hale to drink there as usual and say nothing. If Dessa Quill runs, you'll know who sold the Holts' crossing. "Go," she says. "And girl, come back with the book, not without it."

You shoulder your pack and tell Dreg that two spears is too few for a mountain full of grey cloaks. He takes four. You move fast and quiet along the high road, no fires after dark. You sleep in rotation under the lee of a boulder, with the cold coming off the stone and Dreg's spears taking the watches. Nothing troubles you. The mountain is quiet, as though it were waiting.

By half past six on Day 41, with the sky a deep blue and the first stars out, you come to the edge of Greyshaft. The Foreman's tin-works lie in their cut, with their tall chimneys cold and their gates standing open. Dreg knows the way to the lower galleries, down a stair behind the old ledger-house. At the bottom, a long passage of dressed stone runs to a door with a set of iron scales carved above it. A lamp burns behind the door and shadows move on the crack.

Dreg holds up three fingers, then four. Three or four cloaks, Tolk's rearguard. They have stayed to guard the book, and they don't know their master is taken.

It is half past six in the evening on Day 41. The passage is thirty paces long and the door is shut. What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 171 ==
PLAYER: I signal Dreg and his four spears to hold the mouth of the passage, then walk up to the door alone with the shield low and my sword sheathed, and knock twice, the way Tolk's watcher would have. When it opens, I'll say in Goblin, level and loud, "Tolk is taken, the twenty are done, and the Foreman's dead, so open the book to me and walk out alive."

If any of them reaches for a weapon, I bash the door into the first with my shield and hold the threshold, so only one at a time can reach me and Dreg's spears can come up behind.

DM: You signal Dreg and his four spears to hold the mouth of the passage. Then you walk up alone with your shield low and your sword sheathed, and knock twice, as Tolk's watcher would have. Your knuckles ring on the iron-bound wood. Inside, a stool scrapes.

The door opens a hand's breadth. A cloak with a short sword and a tired face looks out at you and the half-orc's size and the dark behind you. You tell him in Goblin, level and loud: Tolk is taken, the twenty are done and the Foreman is dead, so open the book to you and walk out alive. You hold the tin counting-hand up by its cord so he can see it.

The cloak stares at the token. He looks back over his shoulder. Two more shapes stand behind him in the lamplight, one with a crossbow slack in his hands. For a long moment nobody breathes. You set your foot against the door's edge and keep your shield ready to slam it in if a blade comes up.

None does. The man with the crossbow sets it down on the table. The youngest of the three, hardly more than a boy, says in a cracked voice, "He said the book would pay us, the day he came back." The tired one shakes his head. "He isn't coming back." He steps aside.

The book lies on a lectern under the lamp, a thick leather ledger bound in tin, its pages stained and thumbed. You open it carefully. It is in a tidy Common hand, a column of dates and a column of names. Dessa Quill's initials run down page after page, with years of crossing dates and calving seasons, and the Holt crossing at first frost, one measure of tin. You turn back through the years, and your finger stops at a line fourteen years old. *Goat's Throat, the household of Dargan: path and hour. H. Teyl. No tin paid. Wife held at Greyshaft.*

Hobb Teyl, the old goat-keeper who knew every secret path. The man who sits at Veshra's gate and tells Tessa stories. Dreg reads it over your shoulder, and goes very still.

It is seven in the evening on Day 41. The three cloaks stand with their hands open and the book is in your hands. Nothing in it says whether Hobb's wife lived.

What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 172 ==
PLAYER: I read the line twice and close the book, keeping my voice flat. "No tin paid, and his wife held at Greyshaft, Dreg. That's a man with a knife at his family's throat, not a seller, and I won't hang him on one line."

I turn the pages again for Hobb's wife and any other name marked "held," then tell the three cloaks, "You'll walk to Harrowmere with Dreg's spears and answer to Veshra, not to a mob." I tuck the book under my shield-arm and say, "Nobody touches this but Veshra, Hale and me."

  [game] notebook: updated The Second Ledger
  [game] time: time set: Day 43, 8:00 AM
DM: You read the line twice and close the book, and you keep your voice flat. You tell Dreg that "no tin paid, and his wife held at Greyshaft" is a man with a knife at his family's throat, not a seller, and you won't hang him on one line.

Then you open the pages again and hunt for Hobb's wife, and for any other name marked *held*. The hand is tidy but the ledger is not. The clerk who kept it wrote the held entries in a cramped shorthand of tally-marks and initials, with a different stroke for each year. You turn page after page until the lamp gutters and your eyes ache, and you get nowhere. You can't tell the wife from the cow. The tired cloak watches you from the wall. "Only the Counting-House clerks could read those," he says, very low. "And the boy who read them out each night. Tally-Pip."

You stop. Pip, ink-stained, with flour on his nose, and an even golden flour-cake on a board.

You tell the three cloaks they will walk to Harrowmere with Dreg's spears and answer to Veshra, not to a mob. You tuck the book under your shield-arm and say that nobody touches it but Veshra, Hale and you. Then you sleep in the lower galleries with the door barred and a spear on watch, and at dawn on Day 42 you march south. Nothing troubles the road. It is a quiet mountain, the bound cloaks walk without complaint, and on the morning of Day 43 the roofs of Harrowmere come up out of the frost.

It is eight in the morning. Veshra waits at the gate of her house, leaning on her stick, with Tessa beside her. Hale Orrin leans on the wall, arms folded. Down the street, the smoke from the drovers' tavern rises as usual, and a figure in the doorway is sweeping the step. Behind Veshra's door, you can hear the clatter of Pip's flour-cakes.

What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 173 ==
PLAYER: I hand the tin-bound ledger to Veshra with both hands, tell her briefly what the Hobb Teyl line says and doesn't say, and ask her, Hale and Tessa to read it in the back room with the door shut. "Pip can read the held entries in that shorthand, Aunt. I won't ask a boy to read his own dead master's accounts unless he's willing, so you ask him, gently, and decide."

Then I turn to Tessa and say quietly, "Keep Hobb Teyl at your side today, as if nothing's changed. If he's a man with a knife at his wife's throat, he deserves to be heard before anyone judges him."

  [game] notebook: updated Tally-Pip
  [game] time: 4 hours later: Day 43, 12:00 PM
DM: You hand the tin-bound ledger to Veshra with both hands and tell her briefly what the Hobb Teyl line says and doesn't say. You ask her, Hale and Tessa to read it in the back room with the door shut. Pip can read the held entries in that shorthand, you say, but you won't ask a boy to read his dead master's accounts unless he's willing, so she should ask him gently and decide.

Veshra takes the book as though it weighed more than it should. "Gently," she says. "I've had practice." She looks at you and, for a moment, her hard face is almost soft.

Then you turn to Tessa and say quietly that she should keep Hobb Teyl at her side today, as if nothing had changed. If he's a man with a knife at his wife's throat, he deserves to be heard before anyone judges him. Tessa searches your face. "I'll bring him to the goats," she says. "He'll talk to the goats." She goes.

The back room door stays shut all morning. At last Hale comes out, grey and silent, and sits on the bench with his hands between his knees. Pip comes out after him. He is white to the lips, but he stands straight. "I read them," he says to you, in his small clear voice. "The Foreman made me read them every night. I thought it would be worse to read them for people." He swallows. "It was better."

He tells you only what you need. Hobb's wife, Maret, was held in the Tin Row for two winters and died of a fever in the second. No notice was given. There are eleven other names marked *held*: herders' children and a few men from the lowland steadings who never came home. Veshra wants the families told, but not today.

At noon, the door of Veshra's house opens without a knock. Hobb Teyl stands on the step, an old man in a goatskin coat, with Tessa a pace behind him. His hands are shaking. "I know why you've kept me near," he says in a cracked voice. He looks at you, and at Veshra, and at the closed door of the back room. "I'll say it. All of it. I've waited fourteen years to be asked."

It is noon on Day 43. What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 174 ==
PLAYER: I step aside from the door and say gently, "Then come in, Hobb, and sit, you've carried it long enough." I settle him on the bench by Veshra's stove with warm milk in his hands, take a stool across from him, and say nothing until he's ready.

When he's done, I'll tell him straight what the ledger says about Maret, that she died of a fever, that he couldn't have saved her, and that the line about the pass has "no tin paid" beside it, so everyone in this room can see what was done to him.

  [game] notebook: updated Hobb Teyl
  [game] time: 1 hour later: Day 43, 1:00 PM
DM: You step aside from the door and tell him gently to come in, and sit; he has carried it long enough. You settle Hobb on the bench by Veshra's stove with a cup of warm milk in his shaking hands, and take a stool across from him. Then you say nothing at all.

It takes him a long while. The stove ticks. Veshra stands against the wall with her stick, Tessa sits by the window, and Pip and Hale wait quietly by the door. At last Hobb begins, in a voice that cracks and steadies by turns.

Fourteen years ago, a young hobgoblin sergeant came to his pen at night with a knife and his wife's scarf. Maret had been taken to Greyshaft three days before, and the sergeant held the scarf up and asked for the path and the hour of Dargan's household. Hobb gave it. *Before dawn, the narrow stair.* "I thought they'd take the herd," he says, his hands round the cup. "I never knew he'd hold the pass alone. I never knew." He looks at Veshra. "I came to your gate afterwards. I thought if I stayed near, I might earn it back."

You watch his face the whole way through and read nothing but grief. There is no hedging in him and no pleading. He asks for no mercy.

When he is done, you tell him straight what the ledger says. Maret died of a fever in the second winter, in the Tin Row, and no one told him. The line about the pass has "no tin paid" beside it, so everyone in this room can see what was done to him. There is a long silence. Hobb puts the cup down very carefully, folds his hands, and begins to weep without a sound.

Veshra stands very still. "The sergeant," she says. "Describe him." Hobb does: thin, hard, young, with a way of tapping the flat of a blade on his knee. Tessa makes a small sound from the window. Every one of you knows who that was.

It is one in the afternoon on Day 43. Brakk sits in a lean-to at the Stones, with an arm in a splint. Dessa Quill is sweeping the step of her tavern two streets away. Veshra has not yet said what she will do with the old man.

What does Grunna do?

== ROUND 175 ==
PLAYER: I turn to Veshra and speak low and steady. "Aunt, a man who gave up a path with his wife's scarf under a knife isn't a seller, he's a casualty, and the ledger says so in the Foreman's own hand. I'd ask that Hobb's name be cleared at the thaw, in front of the town."

Then I add, "And the sergeant who held that scarf is in a lean-to at the Stones with a splinted arm. Let me and Ushk go and ask him the plain questions, with Gorrak in the room, before the thaw, and bring his answers straight back to you."
